u .Ji __I_
UTAH-
sent via email: lsimpson@tc-fc.com 
RE: Administrative Amendment - Amended and Restated Development Agreement for the 
Canyons Specially Planned Area, Land Use and Zoning Table for parcel LV-5, file #23-192. 
Dear Ms. Simpson, 
On November 16, 2019, an application was submitted for an Administrative Amendment 
modifying the Amended and Restated Development Agreement for the Canyons Specially 
Planned Area (the "Development Agreement"), Land Use and Zoning Table for Parcel LV-5 
pursuant to Section 5.13.2. The application requests the allowance of four (4') feet of 
additional height for the LV5 parcel. The current Land Use and Zoning Table specifies that 
Parcel LV-5 is allowed a parking/parking garage with a maximum height of 6,780' ASL. Your 
request will amend the Land Use and Zoning Table to authorize a maximum height of 6,784' 
ASL for the parking structure only. Your application states, "As currently designed, the top of 
the Level 5 structural slab is at an altitude of 6770'-0" ASL. The roofs over the stairs are designed 
to 6780'-0" ASL and we have shown elevator overruns per the SPA. Vail requires 9'-0" clear to 
accommodate roof boxes, etc. and the depth of the structural system is 3'. Taking into account a 
flat roof structure, and the use of tapered insulation to provide slope for drainage, it would be 
feasible to construct a roof over the garage with an additional 3' of height though 4' would allow 
for more aggressive drainage slopes. It has been investigated to lower the garage in lieu of 
exploring the Amendment for additional height. This option would result in essentially pushing 
the garage down into a hole that would necessitate the employment of stairs and ramps to access 
the cabriolet and associated plaza. The current design allows patrons to exit out from the garage 
at grade, engaging with the plaza." 
Additional considerations for the additional four (4') feet of height amendment include: 
• Compliance with Dark Skies while ensuring proper lighting for guest safety.
• Furthering the sustainability goals of the Canyons and Summit County by the elimination of
the need for a natural gas snowmelt system on level 5.
• Protection of viewshed by shielding the parking from those uphill from the site.
• Improved safety due to reduced potential for slip and falls.
Please accept this letter as official confirmation that I have approved the Administrative
Development Agreement Amendment for the parcel LV5 change in height from 6,780' ASL to

CANYONS GARAGE – PUBLIC HEARING RESPONSE 
1. TRAFFIC STUDY – (see exhibit)
a. The updated traffic study addresses the traffic impacts associated with the current and
proposed future development of the Canyons Resort.  Included with the study is the traffic
operations and recommended mitigation measures for: 1) Existing conditions plus Project
(Canyons Village Parking Garage) conditions; 2)  Presidents Day (2022) Peak Analysis (with
Project); and 3)  Future 2030 Presidents Day (with Project) conditions.
b. The study used the latest VISSIM Traffic software, which follows The Highway Capacity
Manual (HCM), 7th Edition, 2022 methodology to stay consistent with “state-of-the-
practice” professional standards.
c. It is worth noting that the true impacts of the Project may be lower than is shown in the
study as traffic elsewhere in the resort is likely to be reduced since portions of surface
parking from around the resort will be consolidated into a single parking facility in the Lower
Village.  Due to the inherent difficulty in accounting for this feature, this mitigation was not
assumed, and the analysis is therefore conservative.
i. Existing (2022) Plus Project Conditions (typical winter day)
1. Mitigation Measures – No mitigation measures are needed for existing (2022)
typical winter morning peak hour conditions.  One intersection may operate
at a poor Level of Service (Ozzy Way/Lower Village Road), however, the
volume experiencing this Level of Service is minimal and no improvements
are recommended.
ii. Presidents Day (2022) Peak Analysis (non-typical winter Saturday)
1. Purpose is to study the intersections and roadways during a presumed
worst-case peak travel period for existing background traffic and geometric
conditions plus the net trips generated by the proposed Project assuming
the parking is 100% occupied (no paid parking reduction, thus worst-case
impact).  This analysis period is a typical worst-case benchmark for the
County and reflects non-typical Winter Saturday conditions.
2. Mitigation Measures – Based on peak demand for Presidents Day weekend,
it is recommended that a westbound channelized free right-turn be installed
at the Ozzy Way/Lower Village Road intersection to prevent westbound
queueing from backing into the structure.  The additional lane would merge
before the NW Access.   TCFC will preserve the right-of-way for a free right-
turn onto Lower Village Road, but as stated below, this mitigation measure
is not needed for a typical Winter Saturday, and the Resort will continue to
monitor and update the traffic study on a year-by-year basis after
completion of the parking structure.  This current study did not look at or
incorporate other mitigating transportation options which may present
themselves with additional development within the Lower Village.
Additionally, it is recommended that the outside lane on the northbound
approach of the Frostwood Drive & Lower Village Road/Canyons Resort
Drive intersection be extended back so that vehicles exiting the facility and
drop-off area at the north access may be channelized into their own lane,
allowing free-flow movement.  This improvement at the north access may be
constructed with the garage, if desired.  The parking garage proposal
includes this free right lane out of the garage onto Lower Village Road and
EXHIBIT D
149
helps with pedestrian access and pedestrian crossings.  However, these 
mitigation measures are not needed for typical Winter Saturday conditions. 
iii. Future (2030) Presidents Day Analysis – (2030 was chosen as the future horizon year 
based off of the Snyderville Basin Transportation Master Plan) - (non-typical winter 
Saturday) 
1. The purpose of the future (2030) analysis is to study the intersections and 
roadways during presumed worst-case peak travel periods of the day for 
future traffic and geometric conditions.  This analysis was requested by the 
County to show the impact of future background growth in the area 
unrelated to the Project.  Accordingly, the mitigation measures described 
are driven by background growth and they are not presently required for 
acceptable operation.  While full build-out of all density under the SPA 
Development Agreement is unlikely by 2030, full build-out was analyzed to 
keep consistent with the prior traffic study.

a. White Pine Canyon Road/Lower Village Road 
i. Install Southbound left-turn pocket 
b. Ozzy Way/Lower Village Road 
i. Construct roundabout

i. Stripe “Do not Block Intersection” markings

structure to which a single ply membrane roof will be adhered.  Consequently, there is great 
potential for the addition of future PV arrays.  Employing a ballasted installation system 
would create a simple installation that would be readily maintainable.  The base garage 
design will include spare conduits to the roof to provide a path from the roof to the electrical 
gear for future connections.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 
This study addresses the traffic associated with th e proposed TCFC Parking Structure project 
located in Summit County, Utah. The Project is loca ted on the southeast corner of the Lower 
Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Resort Drive intersection. 
The purpose of this traffic study is to analyze tra ffic operations at key intersections for full-build  
conditions associated with the proposed Project and  to recommend mitigation measures as 
needed. The typical Saturday Winter morning peak hour level of service (LOS) results are shown 
in Table ES-1. LOS A through C are considered acceptable according to County standards. Thus, 
as demonstrated in Table ES-1, the traffic impacts associated with the Project are generally 
successfully within acceptable levels of service in  existing (2022) typical Winter morning peak 
hour conditions, with the exception of the Ozzy Way  / Lower Village Road intersection, which 
operates at LOS D. However, the volume experiencing this LOS is minimal and no improvements 
are recommended. A current site plan of the Project is provided in Appendix A. 
It is worth noting that the true impacts of the Pro ject may be lower than is shown in the study as 
traffic elsewhere in the resort is likely to be reduced since portions of surface parking from around 
the resort will be consolidated into a single parki ng facility near S.R. 224. Due to the inherent 
difficulty in accounting for this feature, this mit igation was not assumed, and the analysis is 
therefore conservative. 
Paid parking is another mitigating feature, because  with paid parking and the ability to control 
pricing, patrons will have additional incentives to use park-and-ride services to access the resort. 
Paid parking has been demonstrated to encourage tra nsit use, improve parking availability, and 
discourage driving alone. This has been shown in resorts in Colorado where a significant portion 
of patrons opted to use external park-and-ride serv ices as paid parking was implemented and 
traffic was reduced by approximately 25%.  
In the Park City Mountain Village parking lots, app roximately 26% fewer vehicles were counted 
mid-morning during the 2022-2023 winter season (when paid parking had started implementation) 
compared to the prior season, which was demonstrate d in the May 2023 Paid Parking 
presentation to the Park City Council 
1. This paid parking mitigation is consistent with t he paid 
parking data from Colorado resorts noted above and is assumed in the analysis. 
 
1 This parking presentation highlights three of the primary benefits of paid parking: (i) Encourages traveling 
by transit; (ii) Improves parking availability; (iii) Discourages driving alone

I.  INTRODUCTION 
A. Purpose 
This study addresses the traffic associated with th e proposed TCFC Parking Structure project 
located in Summit County, Utah. The proposed Project is located on the southeast corner of the 
Lower Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Reso rt Drive intersection. Figure 1 shows a 
vicinity map of the proposed Project. 
The purpose of this traffic study is to analyze tra ffic operations at key intersections for existing 
(2022) conditions with the proposed Project and to recommend mitigation measures as needed. 
 
Figure 1: Vicinity map showing the Project location in Summit County, Utah 
159395
Summit County - TCFC Parking Structure  
Traffic Study 
 
 
2 
 
B. Scope 
The study area was defined based on conversations with the Project team. This study was scoped 
to evaluate the traffic operational performance at the following intersections: 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224 
• 7-Eleven East Access / Canyons Resort Drive 
• 7-Eleven West Access / Canyons Resort Drive 
• Lower Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Res ort Drive 
• Canyons Village Team Building Access & Northwest A ccess / Lower Village Road 
• Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road 
• White Pine Canyon Road / Lower Village Road 
• West Access Road / Northwest Access 
• South Access / Ozzy Way 
• East Access Road / Ozzy Way 
• East Access / East Access Road 
C. Analysis Methodology 
Level of service (LOS) is a term that describes the  operating performance of an intersection or 
roadway. LOS is measured quantitatively and reported on a scale from A to F, with A representing 
the best performance and F the worst. Table 1 provi des a brief description of each LOS letter 
designation and an accompanying average delay per vehicle for both signalized and unsignalized 
intersections. 
The Highway Capacity Manual  (HCM), 7
th  Edition, 2022 methodology was used in this study t o 
remain consistent with “state-of-the-practice” prof essional standards. This methodology has 
different quantitative evaluations for signalized a nd unsignalized intersections. For signalized, 
roundabout, and all-way stop-controlled (AWSC) intersections, the LOS is provided for the overall 
intersection (weighted average of all approach dela ys). For all other unsignalized intersections, 
LOS is reported based on the worst movement. 
Using VISSIM software, which follows the HCM methodology, the peak hour LOS was computed 
for each study intersection. Multiple runs of VISSIM were used to provide a statistical evaluation 
of the interaction between the intersections. The d etailed LOS reports are provided in Appendix 
C. Hales Engineering also calculated the maximum qu eue lengths for the study intersections 
using SimTraffic. The detailed queue length reports are also provided in Appendix C. 
D. Level of Service Standards 
For the purposes of this study, the range of accept able intersection performance levels for each 
of the study intersections is LOS A through D for U DOT roads and LOS A through C for County 
roads. If levels of service E or F conditions (or D  on County Roads) exist, an explanation and/or 
mitigation measures will be presented.

II.  EXISTING (2022) BACKGROUND CONDITIONS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of this section is to identify existing  conditions in the area and provide a baseline 
from which Project traffic may be added and recommendations made. 
B. Roadway System 
The primary roadways that will provide access to the Project site are described below: 
Lower Village Road – is a roadway which is classified as a local road. The roadway has one travel 
lane in each direction. The posted speed limit is 25 mph in the study area. 
Ozzy Way – is a roadway which is classified as a local road. The roadway has one travel lane in 
each direction. The speed limit is assumed to be 25 mph in the study area. 
C. Traffic Volumes 
Saturday morning (8:00 to 10:00 a.m.) and evening (4:00 to 6:00 p.m.) peak period traffic counts 
were performed at the following intersections: 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224 
• 7-Eleven East Access / Canyons Resort Drive 
• Lower Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Res ort Drive 
• Canyons Village Team Building Access & Northwest A ccess / Lower Village Road 
• Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road 
• White Pine Canyon Road / Lower Village Road 
The West 7-Eleven Access / Canyons Resort Drive int ersection was not counted as it is an 
egress-only access, and its volumes were calculated using volumes at surrounding intersections. 
The counts were performed on Saturday, November 19, 2022. The Resort was open on this day. 
The morning peak hour was determined to be between 9:00 and 10:00 a.m. The analysis and 
software tools are set up to analyze a single hour rather than a full 2-hour period. Since the peak 
hour of the 2-hour interval was analyzed, the analy sis is therefore conservative in the context of 
a typical Winter Saturday morning peak period, and if the intersections work during the peak hour, 
they will likely work for the remainder of the 2-ho ur period. Detailed count data are included in 
Appendix B. 
Hales Engineering made seasonal adjustments to the observed traffic volumes to reflect a typical 
Winter Saturday (non-holiday) condition, as requested by the operator. Based on UDOT data at 
the Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224 intersection, volumes on November 19 were approximately 
80% of typical Winter Saturday Conditions. Therefore, the collected data were increased to reflect 
a typical Winter Saturday condition.

III.  PROJECT CONDITIONS 
A. Purpose 
The Project conditions discussion explains the type  and intensity of development. This provides 
the basis for trip generation, distribution, and assignment of Project trips to the surrounding study 
intersections defined in Chapter I.  
B. Project Description 
The proposed TCFC Parking Structure project is loca ted on the southeast corner of the Lower 
Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Resort Dri ve intersection. The Project will consist of 
a parking facility with 1,840 stalls on-site. A con cept plan for the proposed Project is provided in 
Appendix A.  
C. Trip Generation 
Trip generation for the Project was calculated using prior counts performed at the existing parking 
lot on Saturday, February 17, 2018, as typical Pres ident’s Day weekend volumes are usually 
closer to typical Winter Saturday volumes and the occupancy of the existing parking lot during the 
November 2022 count was unknown. 
Based on prior counts in the area, the number of vehicles entering and exiting the existing parking 
lot during the morning peak hour, including cabriol et drop-off, was equivalent to approximately 
65% of the peak parking demand at midday. In these prior counts, approximately 90% of vehicles 
were entering and 10% were exiting during the morning peak hour.  
It was assumed that the vast majority of exiting vehicles were related to the cabriolet drop-off, and 
that approximately the same number would also enter  during the evening peak hour. Therefore, 
approximately 80% of traffic is assumed to be relat ed directly to the parking lot and 20% is 
assumed to be related to the cabriolet during the morning peak hour. 
During the evening peak hour, the number of vehicle s entering and exiting the parking lot was 
equivalent to approximately 60% of the peak parking demand at midday. 20% were entering and 
80% were exiting. 
These assumptions were applied to the new parking facility to be constructed at that parking site, 
increased proportionally according to the counted peak demand of 436 vehicles at the existing lot 
and the planned 1,840 stalls. It was assumed that p eak parking demand without paid parking 
would fill the new facility. 
According to the operator, paid parking has been shown in some Colorado resorts to incentivize 
shifting travel to external park-and-ride services,  reducing the number of vehicles entering the 
resort by approximately 25% while maintaining a sim ilar number of patrons. Paid parking has 
165401
Summit County - TCFC Parking Structure  
Traffic Study 
 
 
8 
 
been demonstrated to encourage transit use, improve parking availability, and discourage driving 
alone.  
Consistent with the results of paid parking in Colorado, in the Park City Mountain Village parking 
lots, approximately 26% fewer vehicles were counted  mid-morning during the 2022-2023 winter 
season (when paid parking had started implementation) compared to the prior season, which was 
demonstrated in the May 2023 parking presentation t o the Park City Council. Therefore, a 25% 
reduction was made to traffic related to the parking facility, corresponding with a facility that would 
be 75% occupied. Analyses assuming a full parking f acility with no reduction are contained in 
Appendix D and Appendix E. 
Trip generation was calculated separately for the p arking facility and the Cabriolet pick-up/drop-
off based on the above assumptions, as shown in Tab le 2. A few token vehicles were assumed 
to be exiting the parking facility during the morning peak hour. 
The total trip generation for the new site with a 25% reduction in parking demand is as follows: 
• Morning Peak Hour Trips:     947 
• Evening Peak Hour Trips:     932 
Table 2: Typical Winter Trip Generation 
 
D. Trip Distribution and Assignment 
Project traffic is assigned to the roadway network based on the type of trip and the proximity of 
Project access points to major streets, high population densities, and regional trip attractions. The 
distribution was also based on existing travel patt erns, which showed a significant amount of 
traffic coming from the west, further in the resort, and turning onto Lower Village Road. This could 
be explained in part by rideshare vehicles elsewher e in the resort. Additionally, parking from 
elsewhere in the resort will be consolidated into this parking facility, meaning that more traffic will 
likely travel to and from the west. The resulting d istribution of project generated trips during the 
morning and evening peak hour is shown in Table 3.

These trip distribution assumptions were used to assign the morning peak hour trip generation at 
the study intersections to create trip assignment for the proposed Project. Trip assignment for the 
Project is shown in Figure 3. 
E. Access 
The proposed access for the site will be gained at the following locations: 
Lower Village Road: 
• The Northwest Access will be located approximately  300 feet southeast of the Lower 
Village Road / Canyons Resort Drive intersection on  the east side of Lower Village 
Road. It is anticipated it will be stop-controlled,  restricted to egress movements, and 
allow right turns only. 
Ozzy Way: 
• The South Access will be located approximately 180  feet east of the Ozzy Way / Lower 
Village Road intersection. It will access the Proje ct on the north side of Ozzy Way. It 
is anticipated that the access will be stop-controlled.  
• The East Access Road will be located approximately  180 feet east of the South Access 
/ Ozzy Way intersection. It will access the Project on the north side of Ozzy Way. It is 
anticipated that the access will be stop-controlled.  
It is recommended that the West Access Road / NW Ac cess intersection (internal) be all-way 
stop-controlled to avoid ambiguity over who has right-of-way.

Traffic Study 
 
 
11 
 
IV.  EXISTING (2022) PLUS PROJECT CONDITIONS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of the existing (2022) plus project analysis is to study the intersections and roadways 
during the peak travel periods of the day for existing background traffic and geometric conditions 
plus the net trips generated by the proposed Project.  
B. Traffic Volumes 
Hales Engineering added the Project trips discussed  in Chapter III to the existing (2022) 
background traffic volumes to predict turning movement volumes for existing (2022) plus project 
typical Winter morning peak hour conditions. Traffi c volumes from the existing parking lot and 
cabriolet drop-off were removed so that they were not double counted. Existing (2022) plus project 
typical Winter morning peak hour turning movement volumes are shown in Figure 4. 
C. Level of Service Analysis 
Hales Engineering determined that all but the Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road intersection are 
anticipated to operate at acceptable levels of serv ice during the morning peak hour in typical 
Winter conditions with Project traffic added, as shown in Table 4. The poor LOS at the Ozzy Way 
/ Lower Village Road intersection applies to a move ment with only two vehicles during the peak 
hour. 
D. Queuing Analysis 
Hales Engineering calculated the maximum queue lengths for each of the study intersections. No 
significant queueing is anticipated during the morning peak hour in typical Winter conditions. No 
queueing is anticipated to extend back from the cabriolet drop-off area in these conditions. 
E. Mitigation Measures 
No mitigation measures are needed for existing (202 2) typical Winter morning peak hour 
conditions with paid parking setting occupancy at approximately 75% as the one poor LOS affects 
a minimal number of vehicles.

APPENDIX D: PRESIDENT’S DAY (2022) PEAK ANALYSIS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of the existing (2022) plus project Pre sident’s Day analysis is to study the 
intersections and roadways during a presumed worst- case peak travel period for existing 
background traffic and geometric conditions plus the net trips generated by the proposed Project 
assuming the parking is 100% occupied (no paid parking reduction, thus worst-case impact). This 
analysis period is a typical worst-case benchmark for the County and reflects non-typical Winter 
Saturday conditions. 
B. Traffic Volumes 
Hales Engineering added the unreduced Project trips discussed in Chapter III without paid parking 
to the existing (2022) background President’s Day evening peak traffic volumes to predict turning 
movement volumes for existing (2022) plus project President’s Day evening peak hour conditions. 
Traffic volumes from the existing parking lot and c abriolet drop-off were removed so that they 
were not double counted. President’s Day background  evening peak hour turning movement 
volumes are shown in Figure 5. Trip assignment for the evening peak hour assuming 100% 
occupancy is shown in Figure 6. Existing (2022) plus project evening peak hour turning movement 
volumes are shown in Figure 7. 
C. Level of Service Analysis 
Hales Engineering determined that the Ozzy Way / Lo wer Village Road, South Access / Ozzy 
Way, and NW Access / W Access Road intersections are anticipated to operate at poor levels of 
service during the evening peak hour with Project traffic added, as shown in Table 4. 
D. Queuing Analysis 
Hales Engineering calculated the maximum queue leng ths for each of the study intersections. 
Some significant queueing is anticipated during the evening peak hour. Maximum queue lengths 
of note during the evening peak hour are summarized as follows: 
 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224: 
o Eastbound: 1,000 feet 
o Northbound: 550 feet 
 
• South Access / Ozzy Way: 
o Southbound: 425 feet 
 
• Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road: 
o Westbound: 225 feet 
 
 
It is worth noting that cabriolet drop-off queues a re not anticipated to back beyond any of the 
parking facility accesses.

E. Mitigation Measures 
Based on peak demand for President’s Day weekend, i t is recommended that a westbound 
channelized free right-turn be installed at the Ozz y Way / Lower Village Road intersection to 
prevent westbound queueing from backing into the st ructure. The additional lane would merge 
before the NW Access. Additionally, it is recommend ed that the outside lane on the northbound 
approach of the Frostwood Drive & Lower Village Roa d / Canyons Resort Drive intersection be 
extended back so that vehicles exiting the facility  and drop-off area at the north access may be 
channelized into their own lane, allowing free-flow  movement. This improvement at the north 
access may be constructed with the garage, if desired. These mitigation measures are not needed 
for typical Winter Saturday conditions.

in presumed worst-case President’s Day conditions ( without accounting for paid parking

APPENDIX E: FUTURE (2030) PRESIDENT’S DAY ANALYSIS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of the future (2030) analysis is to stu dy the intersections and roadways during 
presumed worst-case peak travel periods of the day for future traffic and geometric conditions. 
This analysis was requested by the County to show the impact of future background growth in the 
area unrelated to the Project. Accordingly, the mit igation measures described are driven by 
background growth and they are not presently required for acceptable operation. 
B. Traffic Volumes 
Hales Engineering added the Project trips discussed in Chapter III to the future (2030) background 
traffic volumes, which are based on the 2030 projec tions from the CVMA Transportation Master 
Plan, to predict turning movement volumes for futur e (2030) plus project conditions. These 
volumes are based on full build of the Canyons Spec ially Planned Area (SPA) and volumes on 
S.R. 224 were based on projections from the Synderville Basin Transportation Master Plan. While 
full build of all density under the SPA Development  Agreement is unlikely by 2030, full build out 
was analyzed to keep consistent with the prior traf fic study. Future (2030) plus project evening 
peak hour turning movement volumes are shown in Figure 8. 
As noted in the Executive Summary, the paid-parking  Project is anticipated to have lower traffic 
impacts than has been shown because with paid parking and the ability to control pricing, patrons 
will have additional incentives to use park-and-ride services to carry them to the resort. In some 
Colorado resorts, paid parking implementation has b een shown to shift a significant number of 
patrons to park-and-ride services. This analysis is therefore conservative because it does not take 
into account this traffic mitigation factor, nor do es it account for the consolidation of surface 
parking throughout the resort into the parking faci lity which is anticipated to reduce traffic 
elsewhere within the resort. 
C. Level of Service Analysis 
Hales Engineering determined that many intersection s are anticipated to operate at poor levels 
of service during the evening peak hour in future (2030) plus project conditions. 
D. Queuing Analysis 
Hales Engineering calculated the maximum queue leng ths for each of the study intersections. 
Significant queueing is anticipated at multiple int ersections during the evening peak hour. For 
detailed results, see Appendix C.

E. Mitigation Measures 
The following mitigation measures could be implemented to improve operation in the study area: 
External to Resort: 
• S.R. 224: 
o Widen to a 7-lane cross-section through the study area 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224: 
o Convert third eastbound left-turn lane into a gene ral-purpose lane 
o Install northbound dual left-turn lanes 
§ This will require an additional receiving lane 
o Install an eastbound right-turn pocket and add a r ight-turn overlap phase 
(green arrow) to the signal 
With increased congestion on Canyons Resort Drive and Lower Village Road, it is anticipated that 
some vehicles may choose to use White Pine Canyon Road. If this happens, some of the following 
mitigation measures could be implemented: 
• White Pine Canyon Road / S.R. 224 
o Install traffic signal 
§ In conjunction with the signal, northbound permiss ive-protected left-
turn phasing is recommended 
Internal to Resort: 
• White Pine Canyon Road / Lower Village Road: 
o Install southbound left-turn pocket 
• Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road: 
o Construct roundabout 
• CVTB & NW Accesses / Lower Village Road: 
o Stripe do not block intersection markings 
It is anticipated that additional traffic studies w ill be completed in the future after the opening of  
the parking structure to determine which mitigation  measures may be needed and when they 
should be implemented. 
It is worth noting that Canyons Resort Drive was originally built for 2030 conditions. 
F. Mitigated Scenario 
Even with the proposed mitigation measures above, i t is anticipated that the Canyons Resort 
Drive / S.R. 224, 7- Eleven West Access / Canyons Resort Drive, Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road, 
South Access / Ozzy Way, and CVTB & NW Accesses / Lower Village Road intersections would 
perform at poor levels of service, as shown in Table 7. 
223459
 
 
 
 
1220 North 500 West, Ste. 202     Lehi, UT 84043     p 801.766.4343    
www.halesengineering.com 
 
Despite these mitigation measures, there will still likely be congestion on S.R. 224 and on Lower 
Village Road, and little can be done to further mitigate it.  
As noted, the Project is anticipated to reduce traffic elsewhere in the resort as portions of surface 
parking from around the resort will be consolidated  into a single parking facility near S.R. 224. 
The analysis is conservative because the facility w as assumed to be full. However, with paid 
parking and the ability to control pricing, patrons  will have additional incentives to use park-and-
ride services to carry them to the resort.

Memorandum 
 
Date: 3/19/2024 
 
To: Tiffanie Robinson 
 
From: Brandon Brady, Transportation Planning Deputy Director 
  
Re:  TCFC Parking Structure Traffic Study 
 
After reviewing the traffic study for the new TCFC Parking Structure, staff offer the following 
comments and opinions.  Traffic counts were taken on Saturday, November 19, 2022, when the 
ski season had just started.  Hales Engineering checked to see if this was consistent with a 
typical winter Saturday, and it was not.  It was only 80% of a typical winter Saturday so they 
increased the counts taken on November 19th by 20%.  Staff agree with this seasonal adjustment 
from when they took traffic counts. 
 
When studying traffic in the Canyons area in the past, Summit County has had the consultant 
take counts and analyze traffic on President’s Day Weekend, Saturday after Christmas, and a 
typical Saturday in March.  This is a good sensitivity analysis to assess what might happen on a 
good snow day.   
 
Staff’s opinion is that the assumptions are valid, however, there are concerns about having no 
recommended mitigation measures for the 2022 plus project scenario and the impact this project 
will have on Lower Village Road.  Staff is concerned about the number of vehicles coming out 
of the existing Canyons Resort Drive roundabout and heading southbound on Lower Village 
Road in the AM peak.  Staff are also concerned about the number of vehicles leaving the 
parking structure in the PM peak.  Staff recommends that the following comments be taken into 
consideration with the approval of this project: 
 
1. Free right out of the northwest exit onto Lower Village Road. 
2. Roundabout at Ozzy Way and Lower Village Road. 
3. Preserve the right-of-way for 2-lanes exiting the roundabout going southbound at 
Canyons Resort Drive and Lower Village Road to the northwest entrance. 
4. Complete another traffic study one year after the completion of the parking structure.

separately may be separately analyzed under domestic preference requirements.
28. Accessories sold separately will be separately analyzed under domestic preference requirements. Consult factory for further information.
29. Narrow-band 590nm +/- 5nm for wildlife and observatory use. Choose lumen package D1.
30. Not available in D10 configuration.
31. For installations in locations such as gymnasiums, arenas, sports complexes, multi-purpose rooms, and any other locations where the
fixture potentially will be subject to impacts from external sources, DPM mounting is required, utilizing the stem kit with tether (DPMST*).
Surface Mount, Trunnion Mount (TMB), Wall Mount (WM) and Stem Mount (STM) are prohibited in these applications.

McGraw-Edison
Control Options
0-10V (D) 0-10V dimming comes standard on all TopTier configurations for use with integrated or external lighting controls. 
Dimming Occupancy Sensor (MS/DIM) These sensors are factory installed in the luminaire, dimming to 50% after five minutes of no motion detected. When motion is 
detected, the luminaire output is 100%. Includes an integral photocell that can be programmed for “dusk-to-dawn” operation. The FSIR-100 programming tool can be utilized 
to adjust dimming level, time delay, sensitivity and other parameters. Two lens options provide optimal coverage patterns up to 20’ mounting height. 
0-10V
This ﬁxture is offered standard with 0-10V dimming driver(s). External 0-10V dimming wire leads are provided for use with a lighting control panel 
or other control methods except when PER7, 5LTD, MS/DIM, LWR, IBP or ICP is speciﬁed .
Dimming Occupanc y Sensor (MS/DIM-LXX)
These sensors are factory installed in the luminaire housing. When the MS/DIM-LXX sensor option is selected, the occupancy sensor is connected 
to a dimming driver and the entire luminaire dims when there is no activity detected. When activity is detected, the luminaire returns to full light 
output . The MS/DIM sensor is factory preset to dim down to approximately 50 percen t power with a time delay of ﬁve minutes.
These occupancy sensors include an integral photocell that can be activated with the FSIR-100 accessory for “dusk-to-dawn” control or daylight 
harvesting. The factory preset is OFF. Please refer to the supplementa l guide to understand limitations of produc t use regarding speciﬁc 
applications. The FSIR-100 is a wireless tool utilized for changing the dimming level, time delay, sensitivit y and other parameters.
A variety of sensor lenses are available to optimize the coverage pattern for mounting heights from 8’-40’.
LumaWatt Pro Wireless  Control and Monitorin g System (LWR-LW and LWR-LN)
The LumaWatt Pro system is a peer-to-peer wireless network of luminaire-integral sensors for any sized project. Each sensor is capable of motion 
and photo sensing, metering power consumption and wireless communication. The end-user can securely create and manage sensor proﬁles 
with browser-based management  software. The software will automatically broadcast to the sensors via wireless gateways for zone-based and 
individual luminaire control. The LumaWatt Pro software provides smart building solutions by utilizing the sensor to provide easy-to-use dashboard 
and analytic capabilities such as improved energy savings, trafﬁc ﬂow analysis,  building management  software integratio n and more.  
For additional  details, refer to the LumaWatt Pro produc t guides.

Dimming Occupancy Sensor  (SPB)
These passive infrared (PIR) sensors  are factory installed in the luminaire housing. When the SPB sensor option is selected, the occupancy sensor is connected to a dimming driver and the 
entire luminaire dims when no motion is detected. After a period of time, the luminaire turns off, and when motion is detected, the luminaire returns to full light output. The SPB sensor default 
parameters are listed in the table below, and can be configured utilizing the Sensor Configuration mobile application for iOS and Android devices. The SPB/X is configured to control only the 
specified number of light squares. An integral photocontrol can be activated with the app for “dusk-to-dawn” control or daylight harvesting - the factory default is off. Three sensor lenses are 
available to optimize the coverage pattern for mounting heights from 8’-40’. Four sensor colors are available; Bronze, Black, Gray and White, and are automatically selected based on the lumi -
naire finish as indicated by the table below.
WaveLinx Wireless Control and Monitoring System 
Operates on a wireless mesh network based on IEEE 802.15.4 standards enabling wireless control of outdoor lighting. WaveLinx and WaveLinx Lite sensors utilize the Zhaga Book 18 compliant 
4-PIN receptacle (ZD or ZW), while the WOLC control module utilizes a 7-PIN receptacle. ZW option provides 4-PIN receptacle and control module to enable future installation of WaveLinx 
sensors. ZD option provides 4-PIN receptacle and sensor-ready (SR) driver to enable future installation of WaveLinx sensors, power monitoring, and advanced functionality. WaveLinx (SWPD4 
to SWPD5) outdoor wireless sensors offer passive infrared (PIR) occupancy and photocell for closed loop daylight harvesting, and can be factory or field-installed. Sensors are factory preset to 
dim down 
to 50% after 15 minutes of no motion detected. Two lens options are available for mounting heights of 7’ to 40’. Use the WaveLinx mobile application for set-up and configuration. At least one 
Wireless Area Controller (WAC) is required for full functionality and remote communication (including adjustment of any factory pre-sets). WaveLinx Lite (WOF and WOB) outdoor wireless sen -
sors provide PIR occupancy and photocell for closed loop daylight harvesting, and can be factory or field-installed. Sensors are factory preset to dim down to 50% after 15 minutes of no motion 
detected. Two lens options are available for mounting heights of 7’ to 40’. Use the WaveLinx Lite mobile application for set-up and configuration. WAC not required. WaveLinx Outdoor Control 
Module (WOLC-7P-10A) accessory provides a photocontrol enabling astronomic or time-based schedules to provide ON, OFF and dimming control of fixtures utilizing a 7-PIN receptacle. The 
out-of-box functionality is ON at dusk and OFF at dawn.

WaveLinx-Ready 4-PIN Twistlock Receptacle  (ZW) Includes the WaveLinx control module, integrated 4-Pin receptacle, and standard 0-10V dimming driver, enabling the 
subsequent addition of a WaveLinx sensor. 
Enlighted Wireless Control and Monitoring System (LWR-LW and LWR-LN) The Enlighted control system is a connected lighting solution, combining LED luminaires with 
an integrated wireless sensor system. The sensor controls the lighting system in compliance with the latest energy codes while collecting valuable data about building 
performance and use. Software applications utilizing energy dashboards maximize data inputs to help optimize the use of other resources beyond lighting.
Synapse  (DIM10) SimplySNAP integrated wireless controls system by Synapse. Includes factory installed DIM10 control module and FSP-20 motion sensor; requires 
additional Synapse system components for operation. Contact Synapse at www.synapsewireless.com for product support, warranty, and terms and conditions.

ABB ARBOR 
BOLLARD
 
PATHWAY LUMINAIRE
SPECIFICATION FEATURES
Construction
Top Housing: Low copper, cast 
aluminum top maintains strength 
and precision while providing 
for: rapid heat dissipation, vandal 
resistance and superior dayform. 
Lower Housing: Heavy  0.188" wall 
seamless extruded aluminum 4" 
O.D. shaft attaches to base via 
stainless steel fasteners. BASE: 
Rugged corrosion resistant 
extruded aluminum base mounts 
to foundation with three anchor 
bolts. Base features a pliable 1/2" 
thick neoprene leveling pad fitted 
to the bottom of base allows 
for sealing against water and 
dirt ingress regardless of minor 
deviations in grade of concrete 
pad.
Optics
General purpose symmetric 
distribution is available using 
WaveStream LED optical  
technology. The optical  
waveguide is manufactured using 
precision injection molded acrylic 
for the ultimate level of glare  
control and visual comfort. 
Of
fered standard in 4000K 
(+/- 275K) CCT , optional 3000K 
minimum 80 CRI.
Electrical
LED driver(s) are mounted to 
electrical tray for easy installation 
and maintenance for 120-277V 
50/60Hz, 347V 60Hz or 480V 60Hz 
operation. Offered standard with 
0-10V dimming driver and Cooper 
Lighting Solutions’ proprietary 
circuit module designed to 
withstand 10kV of transient line 
surge. Luminaire is suitable for 
ambient temperature applications 
from -30°C (-22°F) to 40°C (104°F) 
and IP66 rated against the ingress 
of dust and water.
Controls
The Arbor Bollard options are 
designed to be simple and cost-
effective ASHRAE and California 
Title 24 compliant solutions. An 
integrated dimming and occupancy 
sensor is a standalone control 
option available in on/off (MSP) 
and bi-level dimming (MSP/DIM) 
operation. An optional handheld 
remote (ISHH) allows custom 
programming to suit all needs.
 
Mounting
Luminaire is mounted to 3 x 1/2" 
anchor bolts on a 2-3/8" bolt circle 
to with stand a 600 pound overturn 
moment. Order anchor bolts and 
installation template separately 
(ABAnchor).
Finish
Cooper Lighting Solutions utilizes 
premium ultra-weatherable TGIC 
based polyester powder coatings 
that are specifically formulated 
to withstand extended outdoor 
exposure. The powders are 
formulated exclusively for Cooper 
Lighting Solutions to serve 
functionally as well as decorative. 
Good film appearance combinded 
with excellent mechanical an 
exterior exposure qualities 
display greater than twice as 
much gloss retention. RAL and 
custom color matches available. 
Finish is compliant  with ASTM 
B117 3000hr salt spray standard. 
Options to meet Buy American Act 
requirements
Warranty
Five-year warranty.

www.halesengineering.com 
 
MEMORANDUM 
 
Date:  March 20, 2024 
To:  CVMA 
From:  Hales Engineering 
 
Subject: Analysis of Current and Future Anticipated  Demands in Canyons Village with 
the Proposed Lower Village Parking Structure 
UT22-2408 
Introduction 
This memorandum summarizes the existing demands bas ed upon the current ridership data in 
Canyons Village. It also estimates future condition s with the proposed parking garage and 
average wait times for peak hours under normal operating conditions. 
Existing Conditions 
The Cabriolet has a current capacity of approximate ly 2,250 people per hour per direction. 
Cabriolet ridership data was provided to Hales Engineering for the 2023-24 ski season. Based on 
the provided data, the highest Cabriolet ridership hour of the season for uphill traffic occurred on 
February 10, 2024, from 8:00 to 9:00 a.m. with 1,150 people counted. 
This demand of 1,150 people is approximately 51% of  the current capacity. Therefore, there is 
not an existing capacity issue with the Cabriolet i tself for normal operations. Anecdotally, it is 
understood that there are some days in the year where the Cabriolet has queues of people waiting 
to get on. However, this has occurred when there ar e weather or mechanical issues with the 
Cabriolet and is not due to the capacity of the sys tem itself. The current Cabriolet system has 
excess capacity under normal operating conditions. 
Future Conditions 
With additional parking being added with the propos ed TCFC parking structure, it is anticipated 
that Cabriolet demand will increase in future years . As a simple calculation, it is anticipated that 
the increase in demand will may be proportionate to  the increase in parking supply from 1,200 
current stalls in the Cabriolet lot to 1,842 stalls in the proposed structure. This would represent a 
53.5% increase in parking supply and resulting demands. This assumption of growth in Cabriolet 
demand is anticipated to be conservative, as curren t Cabriolet demand also includes transit, 
employee, and pick-up/drop-off usage. Also, the added parking demand in the structure will likely 
03/20/2024
NO. 12335093
JOSHUA D.
GIBBONS
254387
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 1220 North 500 West, Ste. 202     Lehi, UT 84043     p 801.766.4343    2 
www.halesengineering.com 
 
result in more off-peak Cabriolet demand since other Village parking is being reallocated from the 
Village core to the structure. 
Assuming a 53.5% increase, it is anticipated that t he future Cabriolet demand will be 
approximately 1,766 people in an hour going uphill on a peak day. Per wait time estimates from 
Park City Mountain that take into account anticipat ed demands of other hours in the day, it is 
estimated that this future demand could result in an average wait time of 7-8 minutes during only 
peak hours on peak days under normal operating conditions. 
Conclusions 
The findings of this study are as follows: 
• The current capacity of the Cabriolet in the Canyo ns Village is approximately 2,250 people 
per hour per direction 
• The existing highest peak hour demand on the Cabri olet from the 2023-24 ski season was 
1,150 people uphill, which accounts for approximately 51% of the capacity 
• Based on the future demands expected with the prop osed parking structure, it is 
anticipated that the Cabriolet may experience a peak hour demand of approximately 1,766 
people, which is anticipated to result in an averag e wait time of 7-8 minutes during only 
peak hours under normal operating conditions 
 
If you have any questions regarding this memorandum, please contact us at 801.766.4343.

Dear Service Providers,
 
The Community Development Department is in receipt a Low Impact Permit application for Canyons Village Parking Garage, located at 3483 Lower Village Road at Canyons
Village. Per the Canyons Amended and Restated Development Agreement, parcel LV5 is allowed:
 
Project Details:
Project Name:             Canyons Village Parking Garage Low Impact Permit
Project #
         
 
           
 
 
 23-191
Property Owner(s):
    
 TCFC GarageCo LP
Parcel Address:
          
 Approx. 3900 Lower Village Road, Canyons Village
Parcel #:
                     
 
 
 LV5 – (as shown on the Lower Village Development Area Master Plat Amended LV1B and LV5 Subdivision being reviewed simultaneously)
Zoning:
                         
 SPA
Proposed Application: Low Impact Permit
 
The structure is proposed to be 5 levels, with a maximum height of 6784’ ASL.  The applicants have submitted an Administrative Amendment to request the additional 4’ of
height beyond the current allowed maximum of 6,780’ ASL.
 
 The additional height is due to the cap on the upper level which is intended to help comply with dark sky
ordinances while providing necessary lighting for safety, eliminate the need for snow melt on level 5, shield parking for those uphill and improve overall safety due to
reduced potential for slip and falls. The parking will be distributed as follows:
Level 1: 333 Parking Spaces
Level 2: 317 Parking Spaces
Level 3: 355 Parking Spaces
Level 4: 364 Parking Spaces
Level 5: 358 Parking Spaces
Surface: 113 Parking Spaces
Total: 1,840 Parking Spaces
 
The applicant is proposing to construct the garage in two (2) phases, beginning in the spring of 2024 and completing it winter of 2025.   They intend to construct the first
three (3) stories of the structure in 2024 and request a temporary c/o to utilize the first 2 levels of the parking structure for the 2024/2025 ski season the 3rd level would act
as a roof, completing the remainder of the structure by the 2025/2026 ski season. The proposed phasing/temporary occupancy details are included.
 
Your input in valuable and important to the review process. Please provide comments by Wednesday, January 24, 2024. If we do not hear from you by this date, we will
assume you have no concerns with this application. In the meantime, if you have any questions, please feel free to contact me directly.
 
Kind Regards,
Tiffanie N. Robinson, Senior Planner
Summit County Community Development

Hi Tiffanie,
 
Thank you for the opportunity to review the Lower Village Development Area Master Plat.
 
The Transportation Division has the following comments and look forward to working with you as this goes to public
hearing:
We would like to review the traffic impact study and may make comments regarding the impacts and/or mitigation
strategies.
Would the applicant provide additional information on various functions and turning:
What parking technologies will used to provide information to drivers of vacant spaces?
What is the directional turning and signage on L2-L5? Why is it reverse directional?
What kind of staff assistance will there be on heavy-parking days? How are those defined?
We would defer any comments on transit routing (p. 13) to High Valley Transit
 
I apologize for the delay in these comments. Please let me know if you have any questions or would like any
clarification of our comments.

From: Jesse Betebenner
To: Tiffanie Robinson
Subject: RE: Canyons Village Parking Garage LIP - Service Provider Review - #23-191
Date: Tuesday, January 23, 2024 3:25:41 PM
Attachments: image003.png
Hi Tiffanie,
 
The Engineering Department needs the following comments addressed:
1. Please provide a geotechnical report
2. Please provide drainage calculations (Ryan Cathy Letter)
3. Road / Parking Lot Design:
a. Please provide driveway dimensions including widths and approaches (Lower Village Road and Ozzy Way)
b. Please provide driveway slopes on to Ozzy way (must be <3% slope for 100’)
c. Access on Lower Village Road needs to be located minimum spacing of 35’ from opposite intersection (see Summit County Code Section 10-4-10).
d. Please show right of way (including width) of Lower Village Road, Ozzy Way and Canyons Resort Drive.
4. ADA Details:
a. Only 13 ADA stalls are shown on Level 1, plans say there are 14 (as required by Federal ADA code)
b. Please show dimensions of ADA stalls and access isle, both normal and van accessible stalls
c. Please show slopes of ADA stalls and access routes
d. Please show ADA access routes
5. Utility Details:
a. Please call out pipe size and detail on storm drainage system
6. Erosion Control/SWPPP Details:
a. Please show erosion control (silt fence, straw wattle, etc.) on plans.
b. Stabilized construction entrance with specification notes
c. Location of dumpster, toilet, staging area, concrete washout, and construction parking
Please let me know if you have any questions or concerns.  Thank you.
 
Regards,
 
Jesse Betebenner, E.I.T.
Summit County

Hi Tiffanie,
Richard and I have been talking over the proposed parking garage.
 
 We are aware of their intent to build the structure in (2) phases.
 
 We sat down with the architect
last week and discussed many aspects of the project.
  
Typically, the building department doesn't issue temporary C of O's.
 
 However, Richard said he was confident we would be able to accommodate their proposal to
build the first portion and allow it to be used for the season, and then complete the remaining portion the next season.
 
 He just is not sure yet how he'd like to
accomplish it.
 
 As far as the low impact permit is concerned, that's the only issue we're trying to sort out.
Andy Davis

Subject: Canyons Village Parking Garage LIP - Service Provider Review - #23-191
 
Dear Service Providers,
 
The Community Development Department is in receipt a Low Impact Permit application for Canyons Village Parking Garage, located at 3483 Lower Village Road at Canyons
Village. Per the Canyons Amended and Restated Development Agreement, parcel LV5 is allowed:
 
Project Details:
Project Name:             Canyons Village Parking Garage Low Impact Permit
Project #
         
 
           
 
 
 23-191
Property Owner(s):
    
 TCFC GarageCo LP
Parcel Address:
          
 Approx. 3900 Lower Village Road, Canyons Village
Parcel #:
                     
 
 
 LV5 – (as shown on the Lower Village Development Area Master Plat Amended LV1B and LV5 Subdivision being reviewed simultaneously)
Zoning:
                         
 SPA
Proposed Application: Low Impact Permit
 
The structure is proposed to be 5 levels, with a maximum height of 6784’ ASL.  The applicants have submitted an Administrative Amendment to request the additional 4’ of
height beyond the current allowed maximum of 6,780’ ASL.
 
 The additional height is due to the cap on the upper level which is intended to help comply with dark sky
ordinances while providing necessary lighting for safety, eliminate the need for snow melt on level 5, shield parking for those uphill and improve overall safety due to
reduced potential for slip and falls. The parking will be distributed as follows:
Level 1: 333 Parking Spaces
Level 2: 317 Parking Spaces
Level 3: 355 Parking Spaces
Level 4: 364 Parking Spaces
Level 5: 358 Parking Spaces
Surface: 113 Parking Spaces
Total: 1,840 Parking Spaces
 
The applicant is proposing to construct the garage in two (2) phases, beginning in the spring of 2024 and completing it winter of 2025.   They intend to construct the first
three (3) stories of the structure in 2024 and request a temporary c/o to utilize the first 2 levels of the parking structure for the 2024/2025 ski season the 3rd level would act
as a roof, completing the remainder of the structure by the 2025/2026 ski season. The proposed phasing/temporary occupancy details are included.
 
Your input in valuable and important to the review process. Please provide comments by Wednesday, January 24, 2024. If we do not hear from you by this date, we will
assume you have no concerns with this application. In the meantime, if you have any questions, please feel free to contact me directly.
 
Kind Regards,
Tiffanie N. Robinson, Senior Planner
Summit County Community Development

 trobinson@summitcounty.org
Web: www.summitcounty.org

L-100
Landscape Narrative:
The landscape plan for the TCFC Parking Structure will comply with the SPA Design
Guidelines for the Lower Village at the Canyons Village at the Park City Resort. The
plants specified In the plan will be native to the high mountain climate of the resort.
They will be sensitive to creating a natural looking landscape that can be enjoyed by
both people living in the development and passing by. A landscape buffer will be
created between the structure and the existing residential areas. Landscape material
will also be proposed along Canyons Resort Drive.

Canyons Parking Garage – Storm Drain Conformance Review 
Laurel,
We are submitting this letter to confirm that the storm drain design at the Canyons Parking Garage is in 
conformance with the current CVMA Storm Drain Master Plan. The proposed work consists of demolishing 
the existing Lower Canyons Parking Lot, construction of a new parking garage and supplementary service 
lot, and associated road/utility connections. Storm water is currently managed by a single inlet located on 
the northeast corner of the parking lot, which drains to a 24” storm drainpipe that ultimate discharges into 
the Hole 15 detention pond. Stormflows that do not reach this inlet are either discharged onto Canyons 
Resort Drive, the bus drop off loop behind the 7-11 Convenience store, or the Hole 15 golf course.
This conformance review is based on the TCC proposed design as of 02/01/2024. Because the new parking 
garage location sits upon an existing parking lot, it was found that the impervious area from the proposed 
layout is very close to the impervious area that exists now. We have calculated that the new site plan will 
add approximately 11,000 square feet additional impervious area, ultimately draining to the Hole 15 Pond. 
After consideration of the information mentioned above, the master plan model was updated accordingly. 
Results from the model indicate that the 11,000 square feet of additional impervious area will require an 
additional 1,010 cubic feet of detention volume in the Hole 15 Pond during the 100-year, 24-hour storm.
Furthermore, The Utah Division of Water Quality has implemented a requirement to retain and infiltrate 
the 80th percentile storm event. In Summit County this results in 0.47” per square foot. The total disturbed 
area of the project site is 338,882 square feet, or approximately 7.78 acres. It is calculated that 13,273 cubic 
feet of volume must be allocated for the retention burden.
According to the master plan records, the Hole 15 pond has 23,100 cubic feet of unallocated detention 
volume, and 44,887 cubic feet of unallocated retention volume. With approval from the CVMA, the Hole 
15 pond can accommodate the detention and retention burden of the Canyons Parking Garage bringing its 
total unallocated detention volume to 22,090 cubic feet, and its total unallocated retention volume to 31,614 
cubic feet.
Sunrise Gondola Stormwater Calculations Summary:
Detention Volume Required: 1,010 cubic feet.
Retention Volume Required: 13,273 cubic feet.
Total Volume Required: 14,283 cubic feet.
In summary, results from the model show the proposed Canyons Parking Garage stormwater design meets 
the parameters set forth by Ordinance 381-A once volume requirements are met.

1.0 INTRODUCTION
The purpose of this report is to describe the basis stormwater system design for the proposed parking 
garage at The Canyons and provide supporting calculations that demonstrate that the design is 
satisfactory. The project is located at the Canyons Ski Resort, at the intersection of Canyons Resort Drive 
and Lower Village Road in Park City, Utah. 
2.0 METHODOLOGY
The criteria for the storm drain system design are based on Summit County Ordinance 381-A (and 
appendices) whose key design considerations include but are not limited to: 
• Appendix A, page A-15 – Peak Flow Rate Criteria
o Flow rate is not to exceed 10-year and 100-year pre-developed flows.
o Detention ponds are to have a spillway designed to pass the entire 100-year flow.
o The 10-year storm shall be used to size underground pipe conveyance systems.
o All other storm water structures shall be used to safely pass the 100-year storm.
o Only methods identified in Appendix F shall be used for calculations.
• Appendix F – Calculating Peak Discharge and Volume 
o Acceptable calculation methods include the Rational Method and the TR-55 Method.
o Soil data shall be gathered from the website http:/soildatamart.nrcs.usda.gov/ (page 
F-2)
o The runoff Rational C Coefficient tables F1 and F2 on page F-3 shall be used.
o Time of Concentration (T) Equation on page F-4 shall be used
▪ T=1.8*(1.1-c)D0.5/S1/3
▪ Minimum T=10 minutes
The software used to perform the drainage calculations is Bentley Haestad’s SewerGEMS, utilizing physical 
attributes derived from a topographic LiDAR survey, and proposed storm drain design. NOAA Atlas 14 
precipitation intensity tables with a latitude and longitude corresponding to the Canyons Ski Resort was 
used as the basis of storm data within the hydraulic model. See Table 1 below & the Appendix.
Table 1 – NOAA Atlas 14 Powder Mountain Precipitation Data

TALISMAN CIVIL CONSULTANTS, LLC

The storm water model utilizes the Soil Conservation Service Method (commonly known as SCS or TR-55 
Method) to analyze the hydrology of the site. The SCS method is briefly summarized below:  
• The basis of the curve number method is the empirical relationship between the retention 
(rainfall not converted into runoff), hydrologic properties of the watershed, and rainfall 
intensity/distribution. It accounts for most runoff producing watershed characteristics e.g.: 
soil type, land use/treatment, surface condition, and antecedent moisture condition.
• Composite Curve Numbers (CN Values) were developed by reviewing impervious, and 
pervious areas.
• The NOAA Atlas 14 precipitation data was used to create SCS Type II & storm distribution 
hydrograph curves.
3.0 ANALYSIS & RESULTS
Existing Conditions
The “Lower Canyons Parking Lot” currently exists at the project site. The lot is approximately 223,000 SF 
(5.11 acres), made of asphalt and is impervious. The soil classification for the site is found to be Hydrologic 
Soil Group C.  The general topography of the site slopes southwest to northeast at a consistent 6% to 7%.
Storm water is currently managed by a single inlet located on the northeast corner of the parking lot, 
which drains to a 24” storm drainpipe that ultimate discharges into the Hole 15 detention pond. 
Stormflows that do not reach this inlet are either discharged onto Canyons Resort Drive, the bus drop off 
loop behind the 7-11 Convenience store, or the Hole 15 golf course.
Proposed Conditions: Catchment Areas
The site is broken down into 12 catchment areas. Each catchment area was delineated according to the 
existing topography, and proposed grading and drainage plans. The total tributary area directing flow to 
storm drain infrastructure equates to approximately 264,500 square feet. Per Ordinance 381-A, the 
minimum time of concentration of 10 minutes was used for each catchment. 
Each catchment area is characterized by an SCS curve number, with higher numbers indicating a higher 
percentage of impervious surfaces and/or more impervious soils, resulting in increased storm runoff. 
Conversely, lower curve numbers signify reduced runoff, as water either infiltrates directly into the ground 
or encounters vegetation, such as grasses and shrubbery.
The parking structure roof, open air parking lot, road, sidewalks, and other hardscaped areas are 100% 
impervious, with a curve number set at 98. In contrast, the curve number for the surrounding landscape 
is defined at 70, consistent for Type C soils with natural brush weed grass mixture cover types of fair 
condition. 
See the Catchment Tables in the Appendix for a tabular summary of each catchment area including total 
area, SCS curve number and time of concentration. 
4
EXHIBIT H34
300
TALISMAN CIVIL CONSULTANTS, LLC
1588 South Main St, Suite 200, Salt Lake City, UT, 84115 | 801.743.1300 | www.talismancivil.com
Proposed Conditions: Conveyance
The storm drain system is separated into two different sub-networks:
• The Area well, Parking Lot & Roadway sub-network
• Roof Drain sub-network
The two sub-networks are separated by a sand oil/separator located in the emergency vehicle access on 
the northeast side of the garage. Storm water from the Area Wells, Southern Parking Lot, and Roadways 
are routed upstream of the sand/oil separator. The conveyance system from the roof drains are then tied 
into the system downstream of the sand/oil separator.
The proposed pipe sizes vary from 10”, 12”, 18, & 24” in diameter, with a minimum slope of 0.5%. The 
area well drains are  modeled at 10” with a minimum slope of 0.25%. The area well drains are to be 
perforated pipe, typical of wall drainage design. The proposed storm drain system then ties into an 
existing catch basin with a 24” outlet found east of the garage, and ultimately discharges to the Hole 15 
Pond. Results from the model indicate the storm drain design will adequately convey the 10 year, 24-hour 
storm.
See the Conduit Tables in the Appendix for a tabular summary of each pipe area including diameter, slope, 
maximum flow, and capacity. 
 
Proposed Conditions: Retention
The Utah Division of Water Quality has implemented a requirement to retain and infiltrate the 80th 
percentile storm event. In Summit County this results in 0.47” per square foot. The total disturbed area 
of the project site is 338,882 square feet, or approximately 7.78 acres. It is calculated that 13,273 cubic 
feet of volume must be allocated for the retention burden.
According to the master plan records, the Hole 15 Pond Currently has 44,887 cubic feet of unallocated 
retention volume. With approval from the CVMA, the Hole 15 Pond can accommodate the retention 
burden of the Canyons Parking Garage bringing its total unallocated retention volume to 31,614 cubic 
feet. 
Proposed Conditions: Detention
The total existing impervious area from the Lower Canyons Parking Lot is approximately 223,000 square 
feet. The total impervious area from the proposed parking garage and parking lot is approximately 
234,000 square feet, resulting in an additional 11,000 square feet of impervious area.  Results from the 
model show that an additional 1,010 cubic feet of detention volume must be allocated for the detention 
burden.
According to the master plan records, the Hole 15 Pond Currently has 23,100 cubic feet of unallocated 
detention volume. With approval from the CVMA, the Hole 15 Pond can accommodate the detention 
burden of the Canyons Parking Garage bringing its total unallocated detention volume to 22,090 cubic 
feet. 
4.0 Summary
In summary, results from the model show the proposed Canyons Parking Garage stormwater design 
meets the parameters set forth by Ordinance 381-A once volume requirements are met.

1. INTRODUCTION 
 
1.1 GENERAL 
 
This report presents the results of the geotechnical study perf ormed at the site of the proposed 
parking structure to be constructed at Canyons Resort Drive and Frostwood Drive located in Park 
C i t y ,  U t a h .   T h e  g e n e r a l  l o c a t i o n  o f  t h e  s i t e  w i t h  r e s p e c t  t o  existing roadways, as of 2016, is 
presented on Figure 1, Vicinity Map.  A more detailed layout of the site showing the proposed 
building and parking area layout with respect to existing roadways is presented on Figure 2, Site 
Plan.  The approximate locations of the borings completed in conjunction with this study are also 
presented on Figure 2. 
 
1.2 OBJECTIVES AND SCOPE 
 
The objectives and scope of our study were planned in discussions between Mr. Tod Wadsworth 
of Ralph Wadsworth Construction and Mr. Alan Spilker of GSH Geotechnical, Inc. (GSH). 
 
In general, the objectives of this study were to: 
 
1. Define and evaluate the subs urface soil and groundwater cond itions at the site of 
the proposed parking structure. 
 
EXHIBIT H52
318
Ralph Wadsworth Construction 
Job No. 2268-001-16  
Geotechnical Study 
September 29, 2016 
 
 
 
 
   Page 2 
2. Provide appropriate foundation, earthwork, pavement, infiltr ation 
recommendations, and geoseismic information to be utilized in the design and 
construction of the proposed facility. 
 
In accomplishing these objectives, our scope has included the following: 
 
1. A field program consisting of the drilling, logging, and sam pling of 5 borings. 
 
2. A laboratory testing program.  
 
3. An office program consisting of the correlation of available  data, engineering 
analyses, and the preparation of this summary report.   
 
1.3 AUTHORIZATION 
 
Authorization was provided by returning a signed copy of our Pr ofessional Services Agreement 
No. 15-1020 dated September 2, 2016. 
 
1.4 PROFESSIONAL STATEMENTS 
 
Supporting data upon which our recommendations are based are presented in subsequent sections 
of this report.  Recommendations presented herein are governed by the physical properties of the 
soils encountered in the explora tion borings, projected groundw ater conditions, and the layout 
and design data discussed in S ection 2, Proposed Construction, of this report.  If subsurface 
conditions other than those descri bed in this report are encoun tered and/or if design and layout 
changes are implemented, GSH must be informed so that our recom mendations can be reviewed 
and amended, if necessary. 
 
Our professional services have been performed, our findings developed, and our 
recommendations prepared in accordance with generally accepted engineering principles and 
practices in this area at this time. 
 
2. PROPOSED CONSTRUCTION 
 
The project is to consist of a multi-story concrete parking str ucture constructed slab on grade.  
Maximum column and wall loads are anticipated to be up to 575 k ips and up to 8.5 kips per 
linear foot, respectively.  Asphalt parking and roadways surround the structure.   
 
Projected traffic in the parking and primary driveway areas is anticipated to consist of a 
moderate volume of automobiles and light trucks and occasional medium- and heavyweight 
trucks.   
 
Maximum site grading cuts and fills are anticipated to be on the order of 5 to 10 feet.

3. SITE INVESTIGATIONS 
 
3.1 FIELD PROGRAM 
 
In order to define and evaluate the subsurface soil and groundw ater conditions across the site, 
5 exploration borings were drilled to depths of 4.0 and 6.0 fee t below existing grades.  The 
borings were drilled using a truc k-mounted rotary drill rig equipped with hollow-stem augers. 
Each of the borings encountered d rilling refusal in very dense gravel soils.  The approximate 
locations of the borings are presented on Figure 2. 
 
The field portion of our study was under the direct control and  continual supervision of an 
experienced member of our geotechnical staff.  During the cours e of the drilling operations, a 
continuous log of the subsurface conditions encountered was maintained.  In addition, samples of 
the typical soils encountered were obtained for subsequent labo ratory testing and examination.  
The soils were classified in the field based upon visual and textural examination.  These 
classifications have been supplemented by subsequent inspection  and laboratory testing.  
Detailed graphical representation of the subsurface conditions encountered is presented on 
Figures 3A through 3E, Log of Borings.  Soils were classified i n accordance with the 
nomenclature described on Figure 4, Key to Boring Log (USCS). 
 
A 3.25-inch outside diameter, 2. 42-inch inside diameter drive s ampler (Dames & Moore) and a 
2.0-inch outside diameter, 1.38-inch inside diameter drive samp ler (SPT) were utilized in the 
subsurface sampling at the site.  The blow counts recorded on the boring logs were those 
required to drive the sampler 12 inches with a 140-pound hammer dropping 30 inches.   
 
Following completion of drilling operations, the borings were backfilled with auger cuttings. 
 
3.2 LABORATORY TESTING  
 
3.2.1 General 
 
In order to provide data necessary for our engineering analyses , a laboratory testing program was 
completed.  The program included moisture, density, Atterberg l imits, full gradation, and 
chemical tests.  The following paragraphs describe the tests and summarize the test data. 
 
3.2.2 Moisture Tests 
 
To aid in classifying the soils and to help correlate other tes t data, moisture tests were performed 
on selected samples.  The results of these tests are presented on the boring logs, Figures 3A 
through 3E.

4. SITE CONDITIONS 
 
4.1 SURFACE 
 
The site of the proposed construction is an existing Canyons Resort open parking area located on 
the east side of Frostwood Drive and south of Canyons Resort Dr ive and Cabriolet parking area 
ski lift.  The majority of the site surface is covered with asp halt concrete, though eastern and 
southern portions of the lot are covered with gravel.  The surf ace of the site slopes down to the 
northeast with about 15 feet of overall relief.      
EXHIBIT H55
321
Ralph Wadsworth Construction 
Job No. 2268-001-16  
Geotechnical Study 
September 29, 2016 
 
 
 
 
   Page 5 
The site is bounded to the north by additional paved parking, to the south by gravel-covered 
overflow parking, to the east by a golf course fairway and green, and to the west by Frostwood 
Drive paved roadway.   
 
4.2 SUBSURFACE SOIL AND GROUNDWATER  
 
Soil conditions were generally similar across the site.  Fill s oils were encountered in each of the 
borings extending to depths of 1 to 2 feet.  In Borings B-3 thr ough B-5, the fill soils included a 
pavement section including 2 inches of asphalt concrete over fi ne and coarse gravel in the upper 
12 inches underlain by approximately 6 to 12 inches of dark brown, medium stiff, silty clays.  At 
the ground surface in the remaining borings, surficial fill mat erials consisted of light brown, 
dense, fine to coarse sands with some fine and coarse gravel.  The fill soils, considered non-
engineered fills, will exhibit generally poor engineering chara cteristics and are not suitable for 
support of foundations.   
 
Underlying the fills and extending to the depths drilled were red-brown, dense to very dense, fine 
to coarse sandy fine and coarse gravels, which will exhibit mod erately high strength and low 
compressibility under proposed loading.   
 
No groundwater was encountered in the borings during drilling. Seasonal and longer-term 
groundwater fluctuations could occur, with the highest seasonal levels generally occurring during 
the late spring and early summer months. 
 
For a more detailed description of subsurface conditions encountered, please refer to the attached 
boring logs, Figures 3A through 3E.  The lines designating the interface between soil types on 
the boring logs generally represent approximate boundaries.  In  situ, the transition between soil 
types may be gradual. 
 
5. DISCUSSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
 
5.1 SUMMARY OF FINDINGS 
 
The results of this study indicate that the site is suitable fo r the proposed construction.  The most 
significant geotechnical aspects of the site are: 
 
1. The presence of non-engineered fills, including fine-grained soils, which were 
encountered to depths of 1 to 2 feet. 
    
2. Shallow depth to dense granular soils, which resulted in drilli ng refusal across the 
site. 
 
3. The potential for random “perched” water table.   
 
 
EXHIBIT H56
322
Ralph Wadsworth Construction 
Job No. 2268-001-16  
Geotechnical Study 
September 29, 2016 
 
 
 
 
   Page 6 
The proposed structure may be supported upon conventional sprea d and continuous wall 
foundations established upon suitabl e natural soils and/or stru ctural fill extending to suitable 
natural soils.   
 
Non-engineered fills were encountered to a depth of 2 feet and may extend deeper in other areas 
of the site.  Existing road base material may remain under flex ible pavements if properly 
prepared as discussed in Section 5.2.1, Site Preparation, of th is report.  GSH must verify that all 
loose/disturbed soils have been completely removed prior to the p l a c e m e n t  o f  s t r u c t u r a l  s i t e  
grading fills, floor slabs, footings, and pavements.   
 
Due to the shallow depth to dense granular soils resulting in drilling refusal, excavation of the 
subgrade portions of the proposed structures may require heavy machinery.  
 
While groundwater was not encountered during drilling, “perched” groundwater can be expected 
during the winter and spring months.  Below-grade portions of t he parking structure may require 
perimeter foundation/chimney subdrains to control groundwater.         
 
In the following sections, detailed discussions pertaining to e arthwork, foundations, lateral 
resistance, lateral pressures, floor slabs, pavements, the geoseismic setting of the site, and 
infiltration are provided. 
 
5.2 EARTHWORK 
 
5.2.1 Site Preparation 
 
Initial site preparation will consist of the removal of surface  vegetation, non-engineered fill, 
topsoil, pavements, other deleterious materials, and loose/dist urbed soils from beneath an area 
extending out at least 5 feet from the perimeter of the propose d building, rigid pavements, and 
exterior flatwork areas. 
 
Subsequent to the above operations and prior to the placement o f footings, structural site grading 
fill, or concrete slabs, the exposed natural subgrade must be p roof rolled by passing moderate-
weight rubber tire-mounted construction equipment over the surf ace at least twice.  If any loose, 
soft, or disturbed zones are encountered, they must be complete ly removed in footing and floor 
slab areas and replaced with granular structural fill.  In pave ment areas, unsuitable soils 
encountered during recompaction and proof rolling must be remov ed to a maximum depth of 
2 feet and replaced with compacted granular structural fill. 
 
The existing road base material may remain below flexible pavem ents if properly prepared.  
Minimum proper preparation below flexible pavements shall consi st of the scarification of the 
upper 12 inches below asphalt concrete followed by moisture pre paration and re-compaction to 
the requirements of structural fill.  Even with proper preparation, pavements established 
overlying non-engineered fills may encounter some long-term movements unless the non-
engineered fills are completely removed.
