Subgrade preparation as described must be completed prior to pl acing overlying structural site 
grading fills.  Even with proper preparation, pavements established overlying non-engineered 
fills may encounter some long-term movements unless the non-eng ineered fills are completely 
removed.  
 
5.2.2 Temporary Excavations 
 
Temporary construction excavations in the site soils, not excee ding 4 feet in depth, may be 
constructed with sideslopes no steeper than one-half horizontal to one vertical (0.5H:1.0V).  
Temporary excavations up to 8 feet deep in the site soils shall be constructed with sideslopes no 
steeper than one horizontal to one vertical (1.0H:1.0V).  Excav ations up to 12 feet in depth shall 
be constructed with sideslopes no steeper than one and one-half  horizontal to one vertical 
(1.5H:1.0V).  Excavations deeper than 12 feet are not anticipated at the site. 
 
All excavations must be inspected periodically by qualified per sonnel.  If any signs of instability 
or excessive sloughing are noted, immediate remedial action mus t be initiated and GSH 
contacted. 
 
5.2.3 Structural Fill 
 
Structural fill is defined as all fill which will ultimately be  subjected to structural loadings, such 
as imposed by footings, floor slabs, pavements, etc.  Structura l fill will be required as backfill 
over foundations and utilitie s, as site grading fill,  and as replacement fill below footings.  All 
structural fill must be free of sod, rubbish, topsoil, frozen soil, and other deleterious materials.   
 
Structural site grading fill is defined as structural fill plac ed over relatively large open areas to 
raise the overall grade.  For structural site grading fill, the  maximum particle size shall not 
exceed 4 inches; although, occasional larger particles, not exc eeding 8 inches in diameter, may 
be incorporated if placed randomly in a manner such that “honey combing” does not occur and 
the desired degree of compaction can be achieved.  The maximum particle size within structural 
fill placed within confined areas shall be restricted to 2 inches. 
 
Existing sand and gravel soils and fills may be re-utilized as structure fill as long as they do not 
contain deleterious material or debris and meet the requirements of such. 
 
Imported granular structural fill should consist of a fairly well graded mixture of sand and gravel 
with less than 20 percent fines (clays and silts). 
 
Non-structural site grading fill  is defined as all fill materia l not designated as structural fill and 
may consist of any cohesive or granular soils not containing excessive amounts of degradable 
material.

5.2.4 Fill Placement and Compaction 
 
All structural fill shall be placed in lifts not exceeding 8 inches in loose thickness.  Structural fills 
shall be compacted in accordance with the percent of the maximum dry density, as determined 
by the AASHTO

Subsequent to stripping and prior to the placement of structura l site grading fill, the subgrade 
shall be prepared as discussed in Section 5.2.1, Site Preparation, of this report.  In confined areas, 
subgrade preparation shall consist of the removal of all loose or disturbed soils. 
 
Non-structural fill may be placed in lifts not exceeding 12 inc hes in loose thickness and 
compacted by passing constructio n, spreading, or hauling equipm ent over the surface at least 
twice. 
 
If utilized for stabilizing fill, coarse gravel and cobble mixt ures should be end-dumped, spread to 
a maximum loose lift thickness of 15 inches, and compacted by dropping a backhoe bucket onto 
the surface continuously at least twice.  As an alternative, th e fill may be compacted by passing 
moderately heavy construction equipment or large self-propelled  compaction equipment at least 
twice.  Subsequent fill material placed over the coarse gravels and cobbles should  be adequately 
compacted so that the “fines” are “worked into” the voids in th e underlying coarser gravels and 
cobbles.

5.2.5 Utility Trenches 
 
All utility trench backfill material below structurally loaded facilities (flatwork, floor slabs, 
roads, etc.) shall be placed at the same density requirements e stablished for structural fill.  If the 
surface of the backfill becomes disturbed during the course of construction, the backfill shall be 
proof rolled and/or properly compacted prior to the constructio n of any exterior flatwork over a 
backfilled trench.  Proof rolling shall be performed by passing moderately loaded rubber tire-
mounted construction equipment uniformly over the surface at le ast twice.  If excessively loose 
or soft areas are encountered during proof rolling, they shall be removed to a maximum depth of 
2 feet below design finish grade and replaced with structural fill. 
 
Most utility companies and City-County governments are now requ iring that Type A-1a or A-1b 
(AASHTO Designation – basically granular soils with limited fin es) soils be used as backfill 
over utilities.  These organizations are also requiring that in  public roadways, the backfill over 
major utilities be compacted over the full depth of fill to at least 96 percent of the maximum dry 
density as determined by the AASHTO T-180 (ASTM D-1557) method of compaction.  We 
recommend that as the major utilities continue onto the site th at these compaction specifications 
are followed. 
 
5.3 SPREAD AND CONTINUOUS WALL FOUNDATIONS 
 
5.3.1 Design Data 
 
The proposed structure may be supported upon conventional sprea d and continuous wall 
foundations established upon suitabl e natural soils and/or stru ctural fill extending to suitable 
natural soils.  For design, with respect to the proposed constr uction and anticipated loading (up 
to 575 kips for column loads and up to 8.5 kips per linear foot  for wall loads), the parameters 
below are recommended:  
 
Minimum Depth of Embedment for Frost Protection - 42 inches 
 
Minimum Depth of Embedment for Non-frost Conditions - 15 inches  
 
Minimum Width for Continuous Wall Footings - 18 inches 
 
Minimum Width for Isolated Spread Footings - 24 inches 
 
Recommended Net Bearing Pressure for Real Load Conditions

  per square foot

 on suitable granular soils   per square foot

   Page 10 
Bearing Pressure Increase for Seismic Loading - 50 percent 
 
The term “net bearing pressure” refers to the pressure imposed by the portion of the structure 
located above lowest adjacent final grade.  Therefore, the weig ht of the footing and backfill to 
lowest adjacent final grade need not be considered.  Real loads are defined as the total of all dead 
plus frequently applied live loads.  Total load includes all de ad and live loads, including seismic 
and wind. 
 
5.3.2 Installation 
 
Under no circumstances shall the footings be established upon n on-engineered fills, topsoil, sod, 
rubbish, construction debris, other deleterious materials, frozen soils, or within ponded water.  If 
unsuitable soils are encountered, they must be completely remov ed and replaced with compacted 
structural fill.   
 
The width of structural fill, where placed below footings, shou ld extend laterally at least 6 inches 
beyond the edges of the footings in all directions for each foo t of fill thickness beneath the 
footings.  For example, if the width of the footing is 2 feet a nd the thickness of the structural fill 
beneath the footing is 2 feet, the width of the structural fill  at the base of the footing excavation 
would be a total of 4 feet, centered below the footing.  
 
5.3.3 Settlements 
 
Settlements of foundations designed and installed in accordance with the above criteria and 
recommendations supporting the loads can be controlled to within one inch or less. 
 
Approximately 60 percent of the quoted settlement should occur during construction.   
 
5.4 LATERAL RESISTANCE 
 
Lateral loads imposed upon foundations due to wind or seismic f orces may be resisted by the 
development of passive earth pressures and friction between the  base of the footings and the 
supporting soils.  In determining frictional resistance, a coefficient of 0.35 should be utilized.  
Passive resistance provided by properly placed and compacted gr anular structural fill above the 
water table may be considered equivalent to a fluid with a dens ity of 300 pounds per cubic foot.  
Below the water table, this granular soil should be considered equivalent to a fluid with a density 
of 150 pounds per cubic foot. 
 
A combination of passive earth resistance and friction may be u tilized provided that the friction 
component of the total is divided by 1.5.

5.5 LATERAL PRESSURES 
 
A portion of the structure is anticipated to be constructed bel ow existing grade.  Parameters, as 
presented within this section, are for backfills which will con sist of drained soil placed and 
compacted in accordance with the recommendations presented herein.   
 
The lateral pressures imposed upon subgrade facilities will, th erefore, be basically dependent 
upon the relative rigidity and movement of the backfilled struc ture. For active walls, such as 
retaining walls which can move outward (away from the backfill) , drained backfill may be 
considered equivalent to a fluid with a density of 35 pounds per cubic foot in computing lateral 
pressures.  For more rigid subgrade walls that are not more than 10 inches thick, granular backfill 
may be considered equivalent to a fluid with a density of 45 po unds per cubic foot.  For very 
rigid non-yielding walls, granular backfill should be considere d equivalent to a fluid with a 
density of at least 55 pounds per cubic foot.  The above values  assume that the surface of the 
soils slope behind the wall is horizontal and that the granular  fill within 3 feet of the wall will be 
compacted with hand-operated compacting equipment. 
 
For seismic loading of retaining/below-grade walls, the uniform lateral pressures below, in 
pounds per square foot (psf), should be added based on wall depth and wall case:

5.6 FLOOR SLABS 
 
Floor slabs may be established upon suitable natural soils and/or upon structural fill extending to 
suitable natural soils. Under no circumstances shall floor slab s be established over non-
engineered fills, existing pavements, loose or disturbed soils,  sod, rubbish, construction debris, 
other deleterious materials, frozen soils, or within ponded water. 
 
In order to facilitate curing of the concrete, it is recommende d that floor slabs be directly 
underlain by at least 4 inches of “free-draining” fill, such as “pea” gravel or three-quarters to 
one-inch minus clean gap-graded gravel. 
 
Settlement of lightly loaded floor slabs designed according to previous recommendations 
(average uniform pressure of 200 pounds per square foot or less ) is anticipated to be less than 
one-quarter of an inch.

5.7 SUBDRAINS 
 
Due to the possibility of near-surface “perched” groundwater, a  perimeter foundation/chimney 
subdrain system must be installed around all portions of the structure which will extend below 
grade. 
 
The footing drain shall consist of a 4-inch slotted or perforat ed pipe installed on the outside of 
the perimeter subgrade walls to a depth of at least 18 inches b elow the top of the lowest adjacent 
slab.  The subdrain should be encased in “free-draining” gravel , such as “pea” gravel or three-
quarters- to one-inch minus clean gap graded gravel, which shou ld extend 2 inches below and 
laterally from the pipe and at l east 6 inches above the top of the lowest adjacent slab.  The tops 
and sides of the gravel should be covered with a geotextile fab ric, such as Mirafi 140N or 
equivalent, to reduce the possibility of long-term plugging.   
 
Either a synthetic drain board or a minimum 4-inch continuous t hickness of “free-draining” 
granular material should extend upward from the top of the grav el associated with the perimeter 
drain, and to within 2 feet of final drain.  The upper 2 feet of the backfill should consist of a low 
permeability soil, which should slope at least 2 percent away f rom the structure to a distance of 
at least 5 feet.  A sketch of a typical foundation/chimney subd rain system is shown on Figure 5, 
Typical Foundation/Chimney Subdrain Detail (18"). 
 
The subdrain pipe should slope at a minimum of 0.3 percent to a  suitable point of gravity 
discharge. 
 
5.8 PAVEMENTS

areas must be prepared as previously discussed (see Section 5.2.1, Site Preparation).  Under no 
circumstances shall rigid pavements be established over non-eng ineered fills, loose or disturbed 
soils, sod, rubbish, construction debris, other deleterious materials, frozen soils, or within ponded 
water.  Flexible pavements may be established over properly pre pared existing road base as 
previously discussed in Section 5.2.1, Site Preparation.  With the subgrade soils and the 
projected traffic as discussed in Section 2, Proposed Construction, the following pavement 
sections are recommended:

5.9 CEMENT TYPES 
 
The laboratory tests indicate that the natural soils tested con tain a negligible amount of water 
soluble sulfates.  Based on our test results, concrete in conta ct with the on-site soil will have a 
low potential for sulf ate reaction (ACI 318, Table 4.3.1).  The refore, all concrete which will be 
in contact with the site soils may be prepared using Type I or IA cement.  
 
5.10 GEOSEISMIC SETTING 
 
5.10.1 General 
 
Utah municipalities have adopted t he International Building Cod e (IBC) 2015.  The IBC 2015 
code determines the seismic hazard for a site based upon 2008 mapping of bedrock accelerations 
prepared by the United States Geologic Survey (USGS) and the soil site class.  The USGS values 
are presented on maps incorporated  into the IBC code and are al so available based on latitude 
and longitude coordinates (grid points). 
 
The structure must be designed in accordance with the procedure  presented in Section 1613, 
Earthquake Loads, of the IBC 2015 edition. 
 
5.10.2 Faulting 
 
Based on our review of available literature, no active faults pass through or immediately adjacent 
to the site. The nearest active mapped fault is the Salt Lake C ity Section of the Wasatch Fault, 
located approximately 13.8 miles west of the site.

5.10.3 Soil Class  
 
For dynamic structural analysis, the Site Class C – Very Dense Soil and Soft Rock Profile as 
defined in Chapter 20 of ASCE 7 (per Section 1613.3.2, Site Class Definitions, of IBC 2015) can 
be utilized.

period accelerations f or the Site Class B boundary for the Maximum Considered Earthquake 
(MCE). This Site Class B boundary  represents average bedrock va lues for the Western United 
States and must be corrected for local soil conditions.  The ta ble below summarizes the peak 
ground and short and long period accelerations for the MCE even t and incorporates the

5.10.5 Liquefaction 
 
Liquefaction is defined as the condition when saturated, loose,  finer-grained sand-type soils lose 
their support capabilities because of excessive pore water pres sure which develops during a 
seismic event. 
 
Liquefaction of the site soils within the depth penetrated is n ot anticipated during the design 
seismic event due to the lack groundwater and density of the gr anular soils encountered within 
the exploration borings. 
 
Calculations were performed usin g the procedures described in t he 2008 Soil Liquefaction 
During Earthquakes Monograph by Idriss and Boulanger

5.11 STORM WATER INFILTRATION 
 
Stormwater systems installed in the natural sandy gravels encountered in the borings across the 
site can be designed with an infiltration rate of 10 minutes per inch.  A representative from GSH 
must verify the subsurface site  conditions at the final base of  the storm water detention/retention 
basin are suitable and consistent  with those encountered during  drilling so that we can modify 
our recommendations, if necessary. 
 
5.12 SITE VISITS 
 
Due to the presence and variable  nature of non-engineered fill soils at the site, GSH must verify 
that these unsuitable soils have been completely removed and su itable undisturbed native soils 
have been reached prior to the placement of structural site grading fills, floor slabs, footings, and 
pavements.

TECHNICAL MEMORANDUM 
To: Mr. Mark Oakeson, PE 
Wadsworth Brothers Construction 
1350 Draper Parkway 
Draper, UT 84020 
From: Michael Haas, PE 
Reviewed by: Brian H. Garrett, PE 
Travis M. Gerber, PhD, PE 
Date:  December 9, 2019 
Job Number: 19-1222 
Subject: Canyons Parking Structure  [DRAFT] 
 
INTRODUCTION 
This technical memorandum (TM) summarizes findings from our field study and provides 
both temporary construction excavation recommendations and an assessment of the 
suitability of reusing subsurface material as project backfill for the Canyons Parking 
Structure Project located at 4000 Canyons Resort Drive in Park City, Utah.  
 
PROJECT UNDERSTANDING 
It is our understanding that Wadsworth Brothers Construction (WBC) is preparing a cost 
estimate for the design and construction of a new four level parking structure with up to one 
level being located below the existing site grade. We also understand that the parking 
structure will be designed based on the geotechnical design recommendations provided in 
the geotechnical study prepared for this project by GSH Geotechnical, Inc. (GSH, 2016). 
This TM should be considered supplemental to the GSH report and the requested limited 
field study was performed to further assess the subsurface conditions at this site considering 
the test hole auger refusal that was reported by GSH  at depths of 4 to 6 feet below the 
existing ground surface.  
SCOPE OF WORK 
Our studies were performed in general conformance with GC’s Geotechnical Engineering 
Services proposal to WBC dated November 12, 2019.  The scope of services completed 
includes: 
 
· A field study consisting of two test pits as directed by WBC with bulk sampling of 
subsurface soils. 
· Bulk grain size distribution te sting of samples from each test pit. 
· Developing recommendations for temporary construction excavation. 
· Assessing suitability of reusing subsurface material as project backfill. 
· Preparation of this te chnical memorandum. 
 
EXHIBIT H77
343
 
2 
FIELD STUDY 
Test Pits 
Two (2) test pits were excavated for this study on November 15, 2019 (see Figure 1).  The 
test pits were excavated by WBC utilizing a John Deere 310 SJ rubber-tire backhoe.  The 
test pits were excavated to an approximate depth of 8.5 to 9 feet below existing site grades 
for test pits 19-TP-01 and 19-TP-02, respectively, due to caving soils. As directed by WBC, 
test pit 19-TP-01 was excavated northeast of the proposed parking lot in a gravel surfaced 
area near an existing drainage ditch in order to minimize disturbance of the existing parking 
lot. Test pit 19-TP-02 was excavated within the southwest quadrant of the proposed parking 
structure. A bulk sample was obtained from each test pit and transported to our laboratory 
for further testing.  Test pits were backfilled with soil spoils produced while excavating the 
test pits and loosely compacted with the excavator bucket. Some long-term settlement 
above the test pits should be anticipated unless remedial measures are implemented at the 
time of construction. 
 
Test pits were logged by a GC engineer at the time of the field studies. Lines designating 
boundaries between different materials on the logs should be considered approximate; 
transitions between subsurface materials may be gradual or occur between sampling 
depths. Test pit data is summarized in Table 1, and presented graphically in Appendix A. In 
addition to the test pit logs, a legend to soil descriptions have been included in Appendix A, 
and photographs taken during our field studies have been included in Appendix B.  
 
Laboratory Testing 
Grain size distribution testing was performed on a bulk sample obtained from the lower, 
gravel layer (beginning approximately 3 feet below the existing grade and ending at the 
bottom of the test pit) obtained during the field study from each test pit in order to further 
classify the soil and evaluate their engineering properties. Soil particles greater than 3 
inches were manually removed from the soil prior to sampling and testing. Laboratory test 
results are presented in Table 2 and Appendix C. 
 
SURFACE CONDITIONS 
The area of the proposed parking structure is within an existing asphalt parking lot. The 
project site is bound by Canyons Resort Drive on the north and White Pine Canyon Road 
(referred to as Frostwood Drive in the GSH report) on the west, a gravel pit to the south and 
a golf course to the east. The southern portion of the existing parking lot pavement is 
covered with asphalt millings.  
 
SUBSURFACE CONDITIONS 
Based on our field study, subsurface conditions in test pit 19-TP-01 consisted of about 1 foot 
of gravel fill (roadbase) underlain by another approximate foot of silty clay fill. Below the clay, 
the soil observed consisted of sandy gravel with cobbles and boulders. The subsurface 
conditions in test pit 19-TP-02 consisted of about 1 foot of loose asphalt millings underlain 
by approxi
mately 8 inches of intact asphalt pavement. Below the asphalt pavement, the soil 
observed consisted of gravel with sand, cobbles and boulders. The lower gravel layer (that 
gravel beneath the fill and asphalt) in both test pits consisted of approximately 35 to 40 
EXHIBIT H78
344
 
3 
percent cobbles and boulders by volume (as determined by visual estimates), with boulders 
up to 20 inches in diameter.  
GROUNDWATER 
Groundwater was not found in the test pits. Groundwater was also not found in the GSH test 
holes; however,  the GSH report states the possibility of perched water tables during the 
winter and spring seasons.  
 
Groundwater conditions are inherently variable in time and space, changing in response to 
season, climate, precipitation, nearby pumping, and adjacent construction activities. The 
reported groundwater conditions may not be representative of the project site at the time of 
construction.   
 
EARTHWORK 
General 
It is our understanding that the proposed construction will require excavations of up to 12 
feet in the southwest corner of the structure and that structural fill most likely will be required 
along the north and east sides of the structure. We also understand that general grading fill 
(non-structural fill) will be required along portions of the proposed structure’s perimeter.  
None of our recommendations presented in this document are intended to supersede 
recommendations provided by GSH, who remains the geotechnical engineer of record for 
this project.  We recommend that this document be provided to GSH. Because the Test Pit 
19-TP-02 excavation did not extend to the proposed bottom of excavation elevation, there is 
some risk that the soil conditions below the maximum depth excavated, differ from what is 
described in this TM.  
 
Excavations  
The Contractor should rely upon his or her own methods to determine and maintain safe 
and stable excavations during construction subject to his or her particular construction 
procedures and to those subsurface conditions more fully exposed during construction. All 
excavations should comply at a minimum with the Occupational Safety and Health 
Administration’s (OSHA) construction standards. The soil observed in the test pits appear to 
be Type C soils as defined by OSHA.  The Contractor is ultimately responsible for 
excavation, trench and site safety.  
 
During excavation of the test pits, the backhoe operator generally reported relatively easy 
digging conditions. The exception to this was within the lower gravel layer from about 3 to 
4.5 feet in 19-TP-01, where relatively difficult digging was reported. Based on our 
observation of the test pit excavation, it is our opinion that conventional mass excavation 
equipment such as full-size, tracked excavators such as a CAT 320 or larger should be able 
to excavate the site to the proposed depths. Boulders up to 20 inches in diameter were 
found in the test pits; however, based on previous experience in the area, larger boulders 
may be present.  
 
EXHIBIT H79
345
 
4 
Structural Fill 
Based on our laboratory testing, the lower gravel soils (those beneath the fill and asphalt) 
meet the structural fill requirements as defined in Section 5.2.3 of the GSH report, provided 
that the soil is screened on a 4-inch sieve. As previously mentioned, the lower gravel soils 
contain approximately 35 to 40 percent by volume cobbles and boulders (particles greater 
than 3-inches).  However, the grain size distribution testing indicated that as much as 45 
percent of the soil finer than 3-inches consists of particles greater than ¾-inches (referred to 
as oversized particles in ASTM D 4718). It has been our experience with similar soils near 
the project site and elsewhere that compacting and/or demonstrating compaction with 
nuclear density gauges may be difficult. Per ASTM D 4718, the maximum dry density value 
(as estimated by a Proctor test) can be corrected for oversized particles for up to 30 percent 
of the soil. Beyond this amount, the correction is considered invalid. In addition, because of 
the high percentage of oversized particles, larger-sized compaction equipment may be 
required to compact the material to the criteria specified in Section 5.2.4 of the GSH report. 
To reduce these potential compaction related difficulties, we recommend screening out a 
minimum of 20 percent of the particles larger than ¾-inches, in addition to the 35 to 40 
percent of the particles larger than 3 inches.  Alternately, consideration could be given to 
developing revised recommendations to include a method-based compaction criterion. 
 
Non-Structural Fill 
It is our opinion that that the lower gravel soils should, without processing, meet the 
requirements for non-structural fill as defined in Section 5.2.3 of the GSH report. Since 
Section 5.2.4 does not provide gradation or density requirements for non-structural fill, 
screening the soil or utilizing larger sized compaction equipment may not be necessary for 
the placement of this fill. 
 
ADDITIONAL SERVICES 
Subsurface conditions are inherently variable. It is important that we observe subsurface 
materials and conditions exposed at the site during construction, thereby taking advantage 
of opportunities to recognize potentially differing site conditions and reduce the risk of 
unanticipated and/or adverse outcomes. Additional services that we propose include, but 
are not necessarily limited to: 
 
· Observation and testing of site preparation and earthwork activities. 
· Consultation as may be requi red during construction. 
· Temporary shoring design. 
 
We also recommend that we be given the opportunity to review project plans and 
specifications for compatibility with our assessments and recommendations. Additional 
information regarding such services can be obtained from our office
. 
 
LIMITATIONS 
The assessments and recommendations presented in this document are based on limited 
field studies and laboratory testing, as well as our understanding of the project’s design and 
manner of construction.  If the project’s design or manner of construction changes, or if 
EXHIBIT H80
346
 
5 
conditions are found that are different from those described, we should be notified 
immediately so that we can make revisions as necessary. 
 
This document was prepared solely for the use of the addressee and may not contain 
sufficient information for other parties or uses.  Also, this document does not constitute a 
specification and should not be treated or referred to as such in project design drawings or 
documents. 
 
We represent that our services are performed within the limitations prescribed by our Client, 
in a manner consistent with the level of care and skill ordinarily exercised by other 
professional consultants under similar circumstances.  No other representation, expressed 
or implied, and no warranty or guarantee is included or intended.  We do not assume 
responsibility for the accuracy of information provided by others.

he DRC completed their review of the Lower Village Parking Structure project during their

EXHIBIT I2
364
Open House Summary December 22, 2023 
Canyons Village Parking Garage | Open 
Houses Summary 
On November 20, 2023, TCFC held two virtual open houses—one exclusive for CVMA members and 
another for the public—to share information about the proposed Canyons Village Parking Garage 
project. Both events took place on the Zoom Webinar platform. To make the Q&A session more efficient, 
participants were asked to submit their questions beforehand, ensuring the presenters were prepared. 
The project website, containing project information, a video, and renderings of the parking garage 
served as the location to register for the public open house.  
A total of 70 people attended the two open houses, with 21 participants in the CVMA open house at 
noon and 49 attending the public open house at 6 p.m. The CVMA questions focused on ski-related 
facilities, traffic flow, and practical aspects, while the public open house saw diverse questions ranging 
from architectural concerns to financing details, operational logistics, and the long-term impact on the 
community.  
Questions from the open houses were used to update the Frequently Asked Questions (Appendix 1) on 
the project website. The questions are in the following categories: Parking and Traffic Management, 
Sustainability, Safety, Amenities in the Garage, Construction, and Operations. 
CVMA Open House Summary 
The CVMA Open House, held at noon on November 20, attracted 21 attendees. Thirteen questions were 
submitted and asked during the session on the project’s details. 
Attendees showed particular concern for integrating ski-related facilities within the structure, asking 
about the consideration given to incorporating a ski ticket office and Cabriolet loading. Questions about 
traffic dynamics, with specific inquiries about the impact on White Pine Road traffic. Practical 
considerations such as feasibility studies on escalators versus elevators, the accommodation of drop-off 
vehicles, and the functionality of escalators during different times of the day were also asked. 
In addition, attendees asked about the adequacy of elevators, the utilization of the White Pine Road 
entry, the provision of water fountains near the proposed restrooms, and the potential inclusion of 
electric vehicle charging stalls. The session concluded with inquiries about the management of the 
garage, its impact on Cabriolet wait times, and concerns about construction restrictions affecting lower 
resort residents. 
Public Open House Summary 
The Public Open House, held at 6 p.m. on November 20, had an attendance of 49 individuals. Attendees 
submitted and asked during the open house 40 questions, reflecting concerns and considerations from 
the public. 
Questions included topics such as the architectural impact of lighting, the potential height relative to 
adjacent housing, and the perceived community benefits of the project. Attendees expressed concerns 
EXHIBIT J1
365
Open House Summary  December 22, 2023 
about view obstruction, compensation for potential view losses, and the long-term plans for upgrading 
the Cabriolet. Financing and funding sources were also points of interest, with questions asking about 
the private funding nature of the project and the potential contributions from CVMA. 
Practical aspects, including construction logistics, surface parking reconfiguration, and the potential for 
added amenities, were also explored. Attendees also asked for information on the proposed construction 
timeline, the possibility of a second resort entrance, and the impact on pedestrian access during 
construction. The open house concluded with inquiries about the garage's operational details, such as 
sensors for vacant parking stalls, the durability of the exterior, and its year-round operation. 
Promotion of the Open Houses 
The CVMA open house was promoted via email to CVMA members, sharing registration information and 
encouraging their participation. Simultaneously, a news release was sent to the media sharing 
information about the public open house and led to a media inquiry from Park Record.  
In summary, both open houses provided information about the project and allowed the community to 
ask questions.  
EXHIBIT J2
366
Open House Summary  December 22, 2023 
Appendix 1: Updated Frequently Asked Questions 
 
Parking and Traffic Management 
1. Why is the Canyons Village Parking Garage necessary? 
A:  The Canyons Village parking garage is a key obligation under the Canyons Village Specially 
Planned Area (SPA) Agreement and a vital component of the overall transportation infrastructure 
servicing the village. The garage design follows the best practices in the ski resort industry, ensuring 
a world-class arrival experience, consistent with the overall vision of Canyons Village. 
2. How many parking spaces does the garage design offer , and does it increase available parking in 
Canyons Village? 
A:  As designed, the garage and contiguous surface parking lot will provide approximately 1,840 
parking spaces. It is important to note that the garage consolidates and replaces the existing surface 
parking within Canyons Village and results in approximately the same number of spaces currently 
available. 
3. What impact would the garage have on traffic flow in the area? 
A:  Getting vehicles parked and people dropped off more efficiently are key factors in improving 
traffic flow in the area. The garage’s location near S.R. 224 and adjacent to transit was thoughtfully 
selected to make this the parking location of choice for day guests. Multiple access points provided 
in the garage allow for direct access to parking while also providing a covered, 4-lane drop-off area 
for guests. Parking vehicles at the primary entrance to Canyons Village rather than in lots spread 
throughout the area will significantly reduce traffic in the resort core and improve the arrival 
experience. 
4. How will the garage's paid parking lot encourage alternative transportation? 
A:  This project, with its elevated guest arrival experience, provides an opportunity to use tools such 
as paid parking, carpooling incentives, and easy access to public transit to reduce congestion in and 
around the Canyons Village. The specifics of the operating plan for the Canyons Village Parking 
Garage – rates, hours, seasonal variations – will be developed as part of the next phase of the 
project rollout. 
5. Will lights be shining from the sides of the structure? 
A:  Lighting around the Canyons Village Parking Garage has been carefully designed to improve safety 
and aesthetics. The garage was designed to enhance the Canyons Village community and as 
designed, is compliant with the Dark Sky ordinance. 
  
EXHIBIT J3
367
Open House Summary  December 22, 2023 
6. Will the height be higher than the adjacent housing? 
A:  As designed, the garage's height exceeds the Canyons Village Specially Planned Area (SPA) 
Development Agreement by an additional 4 feet, primarily to accommodate the inclusion of a roof. 
The design integrates with the natural contours of the terrain, minimizing visual impact on the 
surrounding area and preserving scenic views. We studied lowering the garage by 4 feet, but 
lowering the garage was incompatible with the Level 1 walkout to the pedestrian plaza. We have 
submitted a request for an administrative amendment to officially incorporate the roof in the 
approved design. 
7. Will there be surface parking available after the garage is completed? Specifically, for users of the 
transit center, will there still be the opportunity and space to park in the lot in the evenings and 
in the summer when skiers are not using the facility? 
A:  The project team is committed to ensuring continued parking opportunities, especially for users 
of the transit center during evenings and the summer season. Specifics related to parking availability 
after the completion of the garage will be addressed in the ongoing planning. 
8. How will the parking garage impact the volume of traffic accessing Canyons Village? 
A:  The implementation of paid parking is expected to reduce traffic volume. Generally, transitioning 
from free to paid parking, carpool incentives, and easy access to public transit are likely to lead to 
decreased volume, especially during peak snow days. Additionally, consolidating existing parking and 
offering multiple entrance points will draw vehicles off the road sooner, enhancing traffic flow and 
efficiency. The specifics of the operating plan for the Canyons Village Parking Garage – rates, hours, 
seasonal variations – will be developed as part of the next phase of the project rollout. 
9. Are changes anticipated to other adjacent local streets? 
A. As of today, there are no additional upgrades planned for Lower Village Road, Ozzy Way, or 
White Pine Road. 
Sustainability 
10. What sustainable design elements have been incorporated into the Canyons Village Parking 
Garage? 
A:  As designed, the exterior of the structure will be covered with beetle-kill lodgepole pine, 
supporting sustainability and reuse. Electric vehicle charging stations are included in this project to 
promote eco-friendly transportation and align with our commitment to sustainability. 
11. Will there be Electric Vehicle Charging Stations? 
A:  Electric vehicle charging stations are planned to be included in the project. 
12. What surfaces will be equipped with snowmelt? 
A:  The roof design promotes the sustainability initiatives of the County and eliminates the need to 
snowmelt the top level of the garage and the entry ramp. For safety reasons, snowmelt is under 
consideration for the pedestrian plaza and surrounding walkways in the next project phase, following 
EXHIBIT J4
368
Open House Summary  December 22, 2023 
best practices for ski resort plaza areas and other recently developed plaza areas within Canyons 
Village. 
Safety 
13. How safe will the drop-off area be within the garage? 
A:  Safety is a priority, and the design focuses on minimizing auto-pedestrian conflicts through an 
internal elevated 4-lane drop-off area on Level 2. 
14. Why were escalators chosen over elevators? Will the escalators only move in one direction in the 
mornings and/or evenings? 
A:  Escalators were chosen based on a vertical circulation study concluding they would move people 
more efficiently than elevators. The escalators will be directional, bringing guests down to the plaza 
in the morning and moving them up in the evening. In addition to the escalators, there are two 
elevators and six full height stairways planned in the structure. 
Amenities in the Garage 
15. How many elevators are there? How many occupants can each elevator handle (with and 
without skis)? 
A:  The parking garage will have two elevators. There is one in the middle of the garage that spans 
the full height of the garage and another in the drop-off area. Escalators and stairs also connect all 
the levels.  
16. Does the garage include amenities like restrooms and drinking fountains, or retail like a coffee 
shop, restaurant, etc.? 
A:  No. Those details will be introduced in Phases 2 and 3. Subject to design and permit approvals, 
the plan is to finish construction of Phase 2 simultaneous with the completion of the construction of 
the garage.   
17. Was any consideration given to incorporating a ski ticket office into this structure? 
A:  A ticket office is something that could be considered in phase 2 of the project after coordination 
with all involved parties. Any suggestions that could make this a world-class arrival experience will be 
considered.  
Construction 
18. What's the proposed construction timeline? Which winter season would this open? 
A:  Pending permit approvals, the construction of the Canyons Village Parking Garage is planned for 
the years 2024 and 2025 with the goal of opening the garage to the public in time for the 2025-2026 
ski season. 
  
EXHIBIT J5
369
Open House Summary  December 22, 2023 
19. Will there be specific times when trucks and the construction crew are restricted from 
operating? 
A:  Details on truck operations and construction crew working hours will be communicated as part of 
the construction management plan. 
20.  What is the plan to accommodate parking while the garage is being constructed?  
A:  This multi-year project will be completed in phases, and construction impacts will be minimized 
during the winter season. Surface parking lots will remain open for parking during construction. 
Public transit is encouraged. Specifics related to the parking plan for the 24-25 Ski Season will be 
communicated once construction of the garage has commenced and the timing of construction 
related impacts can be determined.  
21. Will pedestrian access from Lower Village Road to the Cabriolet be affected during construction?  
A:  Pedestrian access to the Cabriolet from Lower Village Road during the construction process will 
be carefully managed to minimize disruptions. The construction plan will include provisions for safe 
and convenient pedestrian paths, ensuring continued access.  
Operations 
22. Is this 100% privately funded, without the use of tax, assessment, or Canyons Resort Village 
Management Association (CVMA) dollars? 
A:  The project is privately funded, and the Canyons Resort Village Management Association (CVMA) 
has played a crucial role in its advancement. The CVMA partnership has been instrumental in 
addressing critical transportation and development needs in Canyons Village. 
23. Who would manage the garage?  
A:  Park City Mountain Resorts will manage the Canyons Village Parking Garage. Specific details 
regarding management and operational aspects will be determined later .  
24. How will wait times for the Cabriolet be impacted once the garage is completed? 
A. Park City Mountain Resort will review and assess changes to Cabriolet as part of its regular 
planning process. 
 
25. How will the garage work?  
A. The details of the operational plan for the Canyons Village Parking Garage, including rates, hours, 
seasonal variations, equipment storage, gating, parking sensors, and availability indicators, will be 
determined in the next phase of the project rollout.

Subject: Low Impact Permit 
Type of Item:  Public Hearing

I. Recommendation 
Staff has reviewed the application for compliance with all standards in the Snyderville Basin 
Development Code (Code) and found that it does not meet the minimum standards required for 
approval. Staff recommends the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (SBPC) review the 
Low Impact Permit, conduct a public hearing, and vote to forward a negative recommendation to 
the Community Development Director. 
 
II. Project Description 
File #:    23-111 
Project Name:   HF Handyman and Landscaping 
Applicant(s):   Duilio Humberto Fragapane 
Property Owner(s):  Duilio Humberto Fragapane 
Location: 710 Highland Drive 
Parcel Number and Size: HE-B-286, 1.69 acres 
Zone District:   Hillside Stewardship (HS) 
Final Land Use Authority: Community Development Director 
 
III. Vicinity Map 
  
 
372
2 
 
IV. Proposal 
The applicant is requesting approval of a Low Impact Permit for a Home Based Business, Class 
2, for an existing handyman and landscaping business. A Home Based Business, Class 2 is 
defined as: “A commercial or light industrial use of a scale greater than home based 
businesses, class 1 due to factors such as intensity of use or clients coming to the residence, 
but which is still secondary to the residential use.” 
 
Neither a land use permit nor business license have been issued for the existing operation. 
 
V. Process 
A Home Based Business, Class 2 is established through a Low Impact Permit which is typically 
evaluated by the Community Development Director, without a public hearing. Section 10-3-4.C.2 
of the Code states: “In proposals where the CDD or designated planning staff member 
determines that potential issues may arise or additional comment is needed from the community, 
a public hearing on the application may be scheduled with the planning commission.” 
 
Due to complaints from neighbors regarding the on-going, unpermitted business, the 
Community Development Director determined that a public hearing is warranted. 
 
VI. Background 
 
• Parcel HE-B-286 is a legal lot located within the Highland Estates Subdivision.  
• Summit County records indicate the primary residence was constructed in 1995. 
• The property was transferred to the current owner in 2020. 
• Grading of the site began in 2020.

2021 Google Earth    2022 Google Earth          2023 Google Earth

3 
 
• The use of the property appears to be most closely related to “Construction 
equipment storage” which is defined in the Code as: “The storage of any equipment 
used in construction outside the principal or accessory buildings on a property and 
the temporary leasing of such equipment.” 
• “Construction equipment storage” is not allowed in the HS zone district.  
• Staff was informed of the use of the property as a business through complaints from 
adjacent neighbors.  
• Code Enforcement personnel issued a Notice of Violation on June 28, 2023, with the 
option to cease operating at the subject property or apply for a Low Impact Permit for 
a Home Based Business, Class 2, if the owner felt the requisite performance 
standards could be met. 
• The property owner applied for a Low Impact Permit on July 18, 2023. 
• Staff sent an application acknowledgement to the property owner on July 20, 2023, 
stating the application was incomplete. Staff informed the property owner what was 
required to be submitted. 
• Staff received an email from the property owner’s legal counsel on August 4, 2023, 
requesting clarification on the process and submittal requirements.

provisions on August 7, 2023.

property owner intended to comply with the requisite Code requirements on October

have to get that information from the applicant (cc’d here) or applicant’s new counsel 
(if there is new counsel).”

they are out of the country until March 20, 2024.

postpone the hearing because of the length of time that has passed since the Notice 
of Violation was served, the public hearing was noticed, and postcards were sent to 
adjacent property owners. Staff also asked the applicant to provide evidence as to 
how they will comply with the standards of approval.  
 
VII. Snyderville Basin Development Code Analysis and Findings  
A Low Impact Permit is an administrative Conditional Use Permit. Conditions may be added to 
mitigate negative impacts that may be created, but not to compel the applicant to meet the 
required standards for approval. Proof that the applicant will meet the standards must be 
provided. As of the date of this report, no information has been provided by the applicant. 
Staff’s analysis is based on reports and pictures from County Code Enforcement personnel   
  
 
374
4 
 
Home Based Business, Class 2 Performance Standards 
Section 10-8-4 of the Code requires that a Home Based Business, Class 2 comply with the 
following performance standards: 
 
Standard 1. The use of the dwelling unit for a Home Based Business, Class 2 shall be clearly 
incidental and subordinate to its use for residential purposes. Any activity which results from a 
Home Based Business, Class 2 shall be conducted within the enclosed portion of the principal 
residence or accessory building. DOES NOT COMPLY 
 
Analysis: Lot HE-B-286 is 1.69 acres. Approximately 0.83 acres (~50% of the property) 
has been disturbed. It appears the entire area that has been disturbed is used in 
conjunction with the business. Additionally, as of the writing of this report, materials are 
being stored outside.  
 
  
 
 
 
 
375
 
5 
 
Standard 2. There shall be no more than one employee, in addition to members of the family 
residing on the premises, engaged in the activities of the home occupation. DOES NOT 
COMPLY 
 
Analysis: Code Enforcement personnel state that employees are regularly coming and 
going from the subject property.  
 
Standard 3. There shall be no exterior displays, no exterior storage of equipment, including 
unlicensed equipment and materials, and no exterior indication of the home occupation which 
may change the outside appearance of the principal residential character of the building. DOES 
NOT COMPLY 
 
Analysis: As noted in Standard 1 above, as of the writing of this report, materials are 
being stored outside. Additionally, almost 50% of the property has been disturbed. It 
appears the entire area that has been disturbed is used in conjunction with the business.  
 
 
 
Standard 4. No advertising for the home occupation on the premises is allowed, except in a 
mixed use development in a town or resort center and when a comprehensive sign plan has 
been approved which allows such signs. No advertisement of the address of the property to 
attract customers, clients or the public to the premises is allowed. Window areas must not be 
used as display areas to offer merchandise for sale. NO CONCLUSION 
 
Analysis: No information has been provided by the property owner confirming whether 
signs are proposed as part of the Home Based Business, Class 2.

in a residential area. NO CONCLUSION

Low Impact Permit Criteria 
Section 10-3-4 of the Code requires compliance with the following criteria. 
 
Criteria 1. The use does not significantly increase vehicular traffic, unless the increases are 
consistent with previously approved plans for which appropriate mitigation has been 
contemplated and which has been implemented in an appropriate manner to accommodate the 
proposed amendment. DOES NOT COMPLY 
 
Analysis: Code Enforcement personnel state that current business practices result in 
numerous trucks coming and going from the site.  
 
Criteria 2. The use does not significantly increase the demand for parking; unless the increases 
are consistent with previously approved plans for which appropriate mitigation has been 
contemplated and which has been implemented in an appropriate manner to accommodate the 
proposed amendment. NO CONCLUSION 
 
Analysis: Refer to analysis in Standard 9 above.  
 
Criteria 3. The use does not intensify the likelihood of pedestrian and vehicular conflicts. DOES 
NOT COMPLY 
   
Analysis: There is a pedestrian pathway on Highland Drive, across the street from the 
subject property. There is also a bike path located on the same side of the street as the 
residence. The property is located on a curved road and given the number of trucks that 
come and go from the site, Code Enforcement personnel have expressed concerns for 
pedestrian, bike, and car safety.  
 
 
Criteria 4. The use does not create unsightly conditions, including, but not limited to, 
unscreened storage and other environmental concerns. DOES NOT COMPLY 
 
Analysis: Current business practices have resulted in unsightly conditions, including 
unscreened storage of materials and ~50% of the site being graded. Given the

10 
 
disturbance to the site, dust would most likely be created as well as mud and debris

operated, is not consistent with the goals and policies of the General Plan based on the 
following statements in the Plan: 
 
Policy 2.12: Maintain the residential character of existing residential neighborhoods. 
 
Policy 2.16: Future commercial developments should be located in existing commercial 
areas, resort centers, town centers, mixed use areas, or future designated 
redevelopment nodes.   
 
Policy 2.23: Identify appropriate areas for light industrial/service commercial activities to 
meet the needs of local businesses and development activities. Light industrial/service 
commercial activities should be discouraged along scenic corridors or at community 
gateways.    
 
Policy 2.24: Work to develop commercial areas to reduce the impact of: inappropriate 
site layout practices, large parking lot surface, artificial lighting, non-conforming signs, 
and building design. 
 
Policy 5.16: Storm Water: Coordinate with the Summit County Engineering Department 
to encourage sustainable and efficient storm water management practices.   
 
Highland Estates Neighborhod Planning Area 
The neighborhood is predominately characterized by single-family detached residences 
with an average lot size of 1.5 acres. The neighborhood is primarily builtout. A limited 
number of home-based businesses and other commercial activities have established in 
the area which may be inconsistent with the Code and current zoning. A goal of this 
Neighborhood Planning Area is to preserve the existing residential character and ensure 
that incompatible uses are addressed.  
 
VIIII. Recommendation 
Staff recommends the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission review the proposed Low Impact 
Permit, conduct a public hearing, and vote to forward a negative recommendation to the Summit 
County Community Development Director, based on the following Findings of Fact and 
Conclusions of Law.

CONCLUSIONS OF LAW 
1. The use of the dwelling unit for a Home Based Business, Class 2 is not incidental 
and subordinate to its use for residential purposes. The activity which has resulted 
from the Home Based Business, Class 2 is not conducted within the enclosed portion 
of the principal residence or accessory building. 
2. There is more than one employee, in addition to members of the family residing on 
the premises, engaged in the activities of the home occupation.  
3. There is exterior storage of equipment and there is indication of the home occupation 
which has changed the outside appearance of the residential character of the 
property. 
4. The Home Based Business, Class 2 has resulted in equipment on site that is not 
customarily in a residential area. 
5. Open storage, beyond the storage requirements permitted for a residential use, is 
part of the Home Based Business, Class 2.  
6. The Home Based Business, Class 2 contains equipment which creates noise and the 
site has been graded, resulting in dust and mud. 
7. More than one vehicle is utilized for the Home Based Business, Class 2 and they are 
not parked in the driveway or other enclosed parking space, such as a garage. 
8. The Home Based Business, Class 2 has increased vehicular traffic. 
9. The Home Based Business, Class 2 increases the demand for parking. 
10. One of the structures associated with the Home Based Business, Class 2 does not 
comply with Code requirements. 
11. The Home Based Business, Class 2 intensifies noise levels. 
12. The Home Based Business, Class 2 creates dust and dirt conditions. 
13. The Home Based Business, Class 2 is not in compliance with current Engineering 
standards. 
14. The Home Based Business, Class 2 is not generally consistent with the goals and 
policies of the General Plan.

MEMORANDUM 
 
Date:  March 20, 2024 
To:  CVMA 
From:  Hales Engineering 
 
Subject: Analysis of Current and Future Anticipated  Demands in Canyons Village with 
the Proposed Lower Village Parking Structure 
UT22-2408 
Introduction 
This memorandum summarizes the existing demands bas ed upon the current ridership data in 
Canyons Village. It also estimates future condition s with the proposed parking garage and 
average wait times for peak hours under normal operating conditions. 
Existing Conditions 
The Cabriolet has a current capacity of approximate ly 2,250 people per hour per direction. 
Cabriolet ridership data was provided to Hales Engineering for the 2023-24 ski season. Based on 
the provided data, the highest Cabriolet ridership hour of the season for uphill traffic occurred on 
February 10, 2024, from 8:00 to 9:00 a.m. with 1,150 people counted. 
This demand of 1,150 people is approximately 51% of  the current capacity. Therefore, there is 
not an existing capacity issue with the Cabriolet i tself for normal operations. Anecdotally, it is 
understood that there are some days in the year where the Cabriolet has queues of people waiting 
to get on. However, this has occurred when there ar e weather or mechanical issues with the 
Cabriolet and is not due to the capacity of the sys tem itself. The current Cabriolet system has 
excess capacity under normal operating conditions. 
Future Conditions 
With additional parking being added with the propos ed TCFC parking structure, it is anticipated 
that Cabriolet demand will increase in future years . As a simple calculation, it is anticipated that 
the increase in demand will may be proportionate to  the increase in parking supply from 1,200 
current stalls in the Cabriolet lot to 1,842 stalls in the proposed structure. This would represent a 
53.5% increase in parking supply and resulting demands. This assumption of growth in Cabriolet 
demand is anticipated to be conservative, as curren t Cabriolet demand also includes transit, 
employee, and pick-up/drop-off usage. Also, the added parking demand in the structure will likely 
03/20/2024
NO. 12335093
JOSHUA D.
GIBBONS
254387
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 1220 North 500 West, Ste. 202     Lehi, UT 84043     p 801.766.4343    2 
www.halesengineering.com 
 
result in more off-peak Cabriolet demand since other Village parking is being reallocated from the 
Village core to the structure. 
Assuming a 53.5% increase, it is anticipated that t he future Cabriolet demand will be 
approximately 1,766 people in an hour going uphill on a peak day. Per wait time estimates from 
Park City Mountain that take into account anticipat ed demands of other hours in the day, it is 
estimated that this future demand could result in an average wait time of 7-8 minutes during only 
peak hours on peak days under normal operating conditions. 
Conclusions 
The findings of this study are as follows: 
• The current capacity of the Cabriolet in the Canyo ns Village is approximately 2,250 people 
per hour per direction 
• The existing highest peak hour demand on the Cabri olet from the 2023-24 ski season was 
1,150 people uphill, which accounts for approximately 51% of the capacity 
• Based on the future demands expected with the prop osed parking structure, it is 
anticipated that the Cabriolet may experience a peak hour demand of approximately 1,766 
people, which is anticipated to result in an averag e wait time of 7-8 minutes during only 
peak hours under normal operating conditions

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 
This study addresses the traffic associated with th e proposed TCFC Parking Structure project 
located in Summit County, Utah. The Project is loca ted on the southeast corner of the Lower 
Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Resort Drive intersection. 
The purpose of this traffic study is to analyze tra ffic operations at key intersections for full-build  
conditions associated with the proposed Project and  to recommend mitigation measures as 
needed. The typical Saturday Winter morning peak hour level of service (LOS) results are shown 
in Table ES-1. LOS A through C are considered acceptable according to County standards. Thus, 
as demonstrated in Table ES-1, the traffic impacts associated with the Project are generally 
successfully within acceptable levels of service in  existing (2022) typical Winter morning peak 
hour conditions, with the exception of the Ozzy Way  / Lower Village Road intersection, which 
operates at LOS D. However, the volume experiencing this LOS is minimal and no improvements 
are recommended. A current site plan of the Project is provided in Appendix A. 
It is worth noting that the true impacts of the Pro ject may be lower than is shown in the study as 
traffic elsewhere in the resort is likely to be reduced since portions of surface parking from around 
the resort will be consolidated into a single parki ng facility near S.R. 224. Due to the inherent 
difficulty in accounting for this feature, this mit igation was not assumed, and the analysis is 
therefore conservative. 
Paid parking is another mitigating feature, because  with paid parking and the ability to control 
pricing, patrons will have additional incentives to use park-and-ride services to access the resort. 
Paid parking has been demonstrated to encourage tra nsit use, improve parking availability, and 
discourage driving alone. This has been shown in resorts in Colorado where a significant portion 
of patrons opted to use external park-and-ride serv ices as paid parking was implemented and 
traffic was reduced by approximately 25%.  
In the Park City Mountain Village parking lots, app roximately 26% fewer vehicles were counted 
mid-morning during the 2022-2023 winter season (when paid parking had started implementation) 
compared to the prior season, which was demonstrate d in the May 2023 Paid Parking 
presentation to the Park City Council 
1. This paid parking mitigation is consistent with t he paid 
parking data from Colorado resorts noted above and is assumed in the analysis. 
 
1 This parking presentation highlights three of the primary benefits of paid parking: (i) Encourages traveling 
by transit; (ii) Improves parking availability; (iii) Discourages driving alone

A. Purpose 
This study addresses the traffic associated with th e proposed TCFC Parking Structure project 
located in Summit County, Utah. The proposed Project is located on the southeast corner of the 
Lower Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Reso rt Drive intersection. Figure 1 shows a 
vicinity map of the proposed Project. 
The purpose of this traffic study is to analyze tra ffic operations at key intersections for existing 
(2022) conditions with the proposed Project and to recommend mitigation measures as needed. 
 
Figure 1: Vicinity map showing the Project location in Summit County, Utah

The study area was defined based on conversations with the Project team. This study was scoped 
to evaluate the traffic operational performance at the following intersections: 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224 
• 7-Eleven East Access / Canyons Resort Drive

C. Analysis Methodology 
Level of service (LOS) is a term that describes the  operating performance of an intersection or 
roadway. LOS is measured quantitatively and reported on a scale from A to F, with A representing 
the best performance and F the worst. Table 1 provi des a brief description of each LOS letter 
designation and an accompanying average delay per vehicle for both signalized and unsignalized 
intersections. 
The Highway Capacity Manual  (HCM), 7
th  Edition, 2022 methodology was used in this study t o 
remain consistent with “state-of-the-practice” prof essional standards. This methodology has 
different quantitative evaluations for signalized a nd unsignalized intersections. For signalized, 
roundabout, and all-way stop-controlled (AWSC) intersections, the LOS is provided for the overall 
intersection (weighted average of all approach dela ys). For all other unsignalized intersections, 
LOS is reported based on the worst movement. 
Using VISSIM software, which follows the HCM methodology, the peak hour LOS was computed 
for each study intersection. Multiple runs of VISSIM were used to provide a statistical evaluation 
of the interaction between the intersections. The d etailed LOS reports are provided in Appendix 
C. Hales Engineering also calculated the maximum qu eue lengths for the study intersections 
using SimTraffic. The detailed queue length reports are also provided in Appendix C. 
D. Level of Service Standards 
For the purposes of this study, the range of accept able intersection performance levels for each 
of the study intersections is LOS A through D for U DOT roads and LOS A through C for County 
roads. If levels of service E or F conditions (or D  on County Roads) exist, an explanation and/or 
mitigation measures will be presented.

II.  EXISTING (2022) BACKGROUND CONDITIONS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of this section is to identify existing  conditions in the area and provide a baseline 
from which Project traffic may be added and recommendations made. 
B. Roadway System 
The primary roadways that will provide access to the Project site are described below: 
Lower Village Road – is a roadway which is classified as a local road. The roadway has one travel 
lane in each direction. The posted speed limit is 25 mph in the study area. 
Ozzy Way – is a roadway which is classified as a local road. The roadway has one travel lane in 
each direction. The speed limit is assumed to be 25 mph in the study area. 
C. Traffic Volumes 
Saturday morning (8:00 to 10:00 a.m.) and evening (4:00 to 6:00 p.m.) peak period traffic counts 
were performed at the following intersections: 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224 
• 7-Eleven East Access / Canyons Resort Drive 
• Lower Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Res ort Drive 
• Canyons Village Team Building Access & Northwest A ccess / Lower Village Road 
• Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road 
• White Pine Canyon Road / Lower Village Road 
The West 7-Eleven Access / Canyons Resort Drive int ersection was not counted as it is an 
egress-only access, and its volumes were calculated using volumes at surrounding intersections. 
The counts were performed on Saturday, November 19, 2022. The Resort was open on this day. 
The morning peak hour was determined to be between 9:00 and 10:00 a.m. The analysis and 
software tools are set up to analyze a single hour rather than a full 2-hour period. Since the peak 
hour of the 2-hour interval was analyzed, the analy sis is therefore conservative in the context of 
a typical Winter Saturday morning peak period, and if the intersections work during the peak hour, 
they will likely work for the remainder of the 2-ho ur period. Detailed count data are included in 
Appendix B. 
Hales Engineering made seasonal adjustments to the observed traffic volumes to reflect a typical 
Winter Saturday (non-holiday) condition, as requested by the operator. Based on UDOT data at 
the Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224 intersection, volumes on November 19 were approximately 
80% of typical Winter Saturday Conditions. Therefore, the collected data were increased to reflect 
a typical Winter Saturday condition.

Summit County - TCFC Parking Structure

III.  PROJECT CONDITIONS 
A. Purpose 
The Project conditions discussion explains the type  and intensity of development. This provides 
the basis for trip generation, distribution, and assignment of Project trips to the surrounding study 
intersections defined in Chapter I.  
B. Project Description 
The proposed TCFC Parking Structure project is loca ted on the southeast corner of the Lower 
Village Road & Frostwood Drive / Canyons Resort Dri ve intersection. The Project will consist of 
a parking facility with 1,840 stalls on-site. A con cept plan for the proposed Project is provided in 
Appendix A.  
C. Trip Generation 
Trip generation for the Project was calculated using prior counts performed at the existing parking 
lot on Saturday, February 17, 2018, as typical Pres ident’s Day weekend volumes are usually 
closer to typical Winter Saturday volumes and the occupancy of the existing parking lot during the 
November 2022 count was unknown. 
Based on prior counts in the area, the number of vehicles entering and exiting the existing parking 
lot during the morning peak hour, including cabriol et drop-off, was equivalent to approximately 
65% of the peak parking demand at midday. In these prior counts, approximately 90% of vehicles 
were entering and 10% were exiting during the morning peak hour.  
It was assumed that the vast majority of exiting vehicles were related to the cabriolet drop-off, and 
that approximately the same number would also enter  during the evening peak hour. Therefore, 
approximately 80% of traffic is assumed to be relat ed directly to the parking lot and 20% is 
assumed to be related to the cabriolet during the morning peak hour. 
During the evening peak hour, the number of vehicle s entering and exiting the parking lot was 
equivalent to approximately 60% of the peak parking demand at midday. 20% were entering and 
80% were exiting. 
These assumptions were applied to the new parking facility to be constructed at that parking site, 
increased proportionally according to the counted peak demand of 436 vehicles at the existing lot 
and the planned 1,840 stalls. It was assumed that p eak parking demand without paid parking 
would fill the new facility. 
According to the operator, paid parking has been shown in some Colorado resorts to incentivize 
shifting travel to external park-and-ride services,  reducing the number of vehicles entering the 
resort by approximately 25% while maintaining a sim ilar number of patrons. Paid parking has 
165401
Summit County - TCFC Parking Structure  
Traffic Study 
 
 
8 
 
been demonstrated to encourage transit use, improve parking availability, and discourage driving 
alone.  
Consistent with the results of paid parking in Colorado, in the Park City Mountain Village parking 
lots, approximately 26% fewer vehicles were counted  mid-morning during the 2022-2023 winter 
season (when paid parking had started implementation) compared to the prior season, which was 
demonstrated in the May 2023 parking presentation t o the Park City Council. Therefore, a 25% 
reduction was made to traffic related to the parking facility, corresponding with a facility that would 
be 75% occupied. Analyses assuming a full parking f acility with no reduction are contained in 
Appendix D and Appendix E. 
Trip generation was calculated separately for the p arking facility and the Cabriolet pick-up/drop-
off based on the above assumptions, as shown in Tab le 2. A few token vehicles were assumed 
to be exiting the parking facility during the morning peak hour. 
The total trip generation for the new site with a 25% reduction in parking demand is as follows: 
• Morning Peak Hour Trips:     947 
• Evening Peak Hour Trips:     932 
Table 2: Typical Winter Trip Generation 
 
D. Trip Distribution and Assignment 
Project traffic is assigned to the roadway network based on the type of trip and the proximity of 
Project access points to major streets, high population densities, and regional trip attractions. The 
distribution was also based on existing travel patt erns, which showed a significant amount of 
traffic coming from the west, further in the resort, and turning onto Lower Village Road. This could 
be explained in part by rideshare vehicles elsewher e in the resort. Additionally, parking from 
elsewhere in the resort will be consolidated into this parking facility, meaning that more traffic will 
likely travel to and from the west. The resulting d istribution of project generated trips during the 
morning and evening peak hour is shown in Table 3.

These trip distribution assumptions were used to assign the morning peak hour trip generation at 
the study intersections to create trip assignment for the proposed Project. Trip assignment for the 
Project is shown in Figure 3. 
E. Access 
The proposed access for the site will be gained at the following locations: 
Lower Village Road: 
• The Northwest Access will be located approximately  300 feet southeast of the Lower 
Village Road / Canyons Resort Drive intersection on  the east side of Lower Village 
Road. It is anticipated it will be stop-controlled,  restricted to egress movements, and 
allow right turns only. 
Ozzy Way: 
• The South Access will be located approximately 180  feet east of the Ozzy Way / Lower 
Village Road intersection. It will access the Proje ct on the north side of Ozzy Way. It 
is anticipated that the access will be stop-controlled.  
• The East Access Road will be located approximately  180 feet east of the South Access 
/ Ozzy Way intersection. It will access the Project on the north side of Ozzy Way. It is 
anticipated that the access will be stop-controlled.  
It is recommended that the West Access Road / NW Ac cess intersection (internal) be all-way 
stop-controlled to avoid ambiguity over who has right-of-way.

IV.  EXISTING (2022) PLUS PROJECT CONDITIONS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of the existing (2022) plus project analysis is to study the intersections and roadways 
during the peak travel periods of the day for existing background traffic and geometric conditions 
plus the net trips generated by the proposed Project.  
B. Traffic Volumes 
Hales Engineering added the Project trips discussed  in Chapter III to the existing (2022) 
background traffic volumes to predict turning movement volumes for existing (2022) plus project 
typical Winter morning peak hour conditions. Traffi c volumes from the existing parking lot and 
cabriolet drop-off were removed so that they were not double counted. Existing (2022) plus project 
typical Winter morning peak hour turning movement volumes are shown in Figure 4. 
C. Level of Service Analysis 
Hales Engineering determined that all but the Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road intersection are 
anticipated to operate at acceptable levels of serv ice during the morning peak hour in typical 
Winter conditions with Project traffic added, as shown in Table 4. The poor LOS at the Ozzy Way 
/ Lower Village Road intersection applies to a move ment with only two vehicles during the peak 
hour. 
D. Queuing Analysis 
Hales Engineering calculated the maximum queue lengths for each of the study intersections. No 
significant queueing is anticipated during the morning peak hour in typical Winter conditions. No 
queueing is anticipated to extend back from the cabriolet drop-off area in these conditions. 
E. Mitigation Measures 
No mitigation measures are needed for existing (202 2) typical Winter morning peak hour 
conditions with paid parking setting occupancy at approximately 75% as the one poor LOS affects 
a minimal number of vehicles.

APPENDIX D: PRESIDENT’S DAY (2022) PEAK ANALYSIS 
A. Purpose 
The purpose of the existing (2022) plus project Pre sident’s Day analysis is to study the 
intersections and roadways during a presumed worst- case peak travel period for existing 
background traffic and geometric conditions plus the net trips generated by the proposed Project 
assuming the parking is 100% occupied (no paid parking reduction, thus worst-case impact). This 
analysis period is a typical worst-case benchmark for the County and reflects non-typical Winter 
Saturday conditions. 
B. Traffic Volumes 
Hales Engineering added the unreduced Project trips discussed in Chapter III without paid parking 
to the existing (2022) background President’s Day evening peak traffic volumes to predict turning 
movement volumes for existing (2022) plus project President’s Day evening peak hour conditions. 
Traffic volumes from the existing parking lot and c abriolet drop-off were removed so that they 
were not double counted. President’s Day background  evening peak hour turning movement 
volumes are shown in Figure 5. Trip assignment for the evening peak hour assuming 100% 
occupancy is shown in Figure 6. Existing (2022) plus project evening peak hour turning movement 
volumes are shown in Figure 7. 
C. Level of Service Analysis 
Hales Engineering determined that the Ozzy Way / Lo wer Village Road, South Access / Ozzy 
Way, and NW Access / W Access Road intersections are anticipated to operate at poor levels of 
service during the evening peak hour with Project traffic added, as shown in Table 4. 
D. Queuing Analysis 
Hales Engineering calculated the maximum queue leng ths for each of the study intersections. 
Some significant queueing is anticipated during the evening peak hour. Maximum queue lengths 
of note during the evening peak hour are summarized as follows: 
 
• Canyons Resort Drive / S.R. 224: 
o Eastbound: 1,000 feet 
o Northbound: 550 feet 
 
• South Access / Ozzy Way: 
o Southbound: 425 feet 
 
• Ozzy Way / Lower Village Road: 
o Westbound: 225 feet 
 
 
It is worth noting that cabriolet drop-off queues a re not anticipated to back beyond any of the 
parking facility accesses.

E. Mitigation Measures 
Based on peak demand for President’s Day weekend, i t is recommended that a westbound 
channelized free right-turn be installed at the Ozz y Way / Lower Village Road intersection to 
prevent westbound queueing from backing into the st ructure. The additional lane would merge 
before the NW Access. Additionally, it is recommend ed that the outside lane on the northbound 
approach of the Frostwood Drive & Lower Village Roa d / Canyons Resort Drive intersection be 
extended back so that vehicles exiting the facility  and drop-off area at the north access may be 
channelized into their own lane, allowing free-flow  movement. This improvement at the north 
access may be constructed with the garage, if desired. These mitigation measures are not needed 
for typical Winter Saturday conditions.

www.halesengineering.com 
 
F. Mitigated Scenario 
With the proposed improvement described herein, it is anticipated that all study intersections (with 
the exception of the 7-Eleven West Access / Canyon Resort Drive and South Access / Ozzy Way 
intersections, which cannot be mitigated) will successfully operate at acceptable levels of service 
in presumed worst-case President’s Day conditions ( without accounting for paid parking 
mitigation), as shown in Table 6.
